<a id='question-067'></a>

### Question 067: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 17 (affects)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular defect in Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS), a primary immunodeficiency characterized by eczema, thrombocytopenia, and recurrent infections, specifically focusing on the role of the WAS protein (WASP) in actin cytoskeleton regulation and immune cell signaling. (本题考察Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS）的分子缺陷，这是一种原发性免疫缺陷病，其特征是湿疹、血小板减少症和复发性感染，重点考察WASP蛋白在actin细胞骨架调控和免疫细胞信号传导中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the pediatrician with a history of severe, persistent eczema since infancy, frequent bleeding episodes (bruising, nosebleeds), and recurrent respiratory infections (otitis media, pneumonia). His mother reports that he has small platelets on his routine blood counts. Family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died at age 10 from lymphoma. Physical examination reveals generalized eczematous lesions and petechiae on his lower extremities. Laboratory findings show thrombocytopenia (platelet count 25,000/µL) with small mean platelet volume (MPV), T-cell lymphopenia (CD4+ T cells 200/µL, CD8+ T cells 150/µL), and low levels of IgM. Genetic testing reveals a mutation in the *WAS* gene located on the X chromosome.

Which of the following molecular pathways is most directly affected by the mutation in the *WAS* gene, leading to the clinical manifestations observed in this patient?

(A) The complement cascade, specifically the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC).
(B) The actin cytoskeleton organization and T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling.
(C) The B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling pathway, leading to impaired isotype switching.
(D) The phagocytic pathway, specifically the oxidative burst mediated by NADPH oxidase.
(E) The interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) signaling pathway, crucial for macrophage activation.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy. This age is typical for the onset of symptoms in Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS).
- **Clinical Triad:** Eczema (severe, persistent since infancy), Thrombocytopenia (frequent bleeding, small platelets, low MPV), Recurrent Infections (respiratory). This triad is highly suggestive of WAS.
- **Family History:** Maternal uncle died young from lymphoma. WAS patients have an increased risk of autoimmunity and malignancy, particularly lymphoma. The X-linked inheritance pattern is also suggested by the affected maternal uncle.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Thrombocytopenia with small platelets (microthrombocytopenia), T-cell lymphopenia (low CD4+ and CD8+ counts), low IgM. These findings are characteristic of WAS.
- **Genetic Testing:** Mutation in the *WAS* gene on the X chromosome confirms the diagnosis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular pathway directly affected by the *WAS* gene mutation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS):** An X-linked recessive primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene.
- **WAS Protein (WASP):** A cytoplasmic protein expressed in hematopoietic cells, particularly T lymphocytes, B lymphocytes, platelets, and hematopoietic stem cells.
- **Function of WASP:** WASP is a crucial regulator of the actin cytoskeleton. It interacts with various signaling molecules and actin-binding proteins (like Arp2/3 complex, WAVE/Scar complex, N-WASP) to control actin polymerization, branching, and cell motility.
- **Role in T-cell Signaling:** WASP is essential for proper T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling. Upon TCR engagement, WASP is recruited to the immunological synapse, where it promotes actin rearrangement, leading to the formation of microvilli (immune synapse structure) and efficient signal transduction. This is critical for T-cell activation, proliferation, and migration.
- **Role in Platelets:** WASP is involved in platelet formation (megakaryopoiesis) and function. Mutations lead to defective platelet production (resulting in thrombocytopenia) and abnormal platelet size (small platelets, low MPV) and function.
- **Role in B-cells:** WASP is involved in B-cell development and function, contributing to impaired antibody responses, particularly IgM production.
- **Pathophysiology:** The lack of functional WASP disrupts actin cytoskeleton dynamics, impairing T-cell activation and migration, platelet development and function, and B-cell function. This leads to the characteristic clinical features: impaired T-cell immunity (recurrent infections), defective platelet function/number (bleeding), and eczema (mechanism less clear, possibly related to T-cell dysregulation or impaired skin barrier function).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **The complement cascade, specifically the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC).** Defects in the complement cascade (e.g., C5-C9 deficiencies) lead to increased susceptibility to *Neisseria* infections, not the triad seen in WAS. WAS does not primarily affect complement function.
- (C) **The B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling pathway, leading to impaired isotype switching.** While WAS affects B-cell function (low IgM), the primary defect is not in the BCR signaling pathway itself, but rather in the broader actin cytoskeleton regulation required for lymphocyte function. Hyper-IgM syndrome involves defects in CD40L or CD40, or AID, which directly impair isotype switching.
- (D) **The phagocytic pathway, specifically the oxidative burst mediated by NADPH oxidase.** Defects in NADPH oxidase cause Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD), characterized by recurrent bacterial and fungal infections (especially catalase-positive organisms) and granuloma formation. This is distinct from the clinical presentation of WAS.
- (E) **The interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) signaling pathway, crucial for macrophage activation.** Defects in IFN-γ signaling (e.g., mutations in IFNGR1/IFNGR2) or production (e.g., IL-12/IFN-γ pathway defects) lead to susceptibility to intracellular pathogens like mycobacteria and *Salmonella*. This is not the primary defect in WAS.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS) | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | Hyper-IgM Syndrome | Complement Deficiency |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|--------------------------------------|----------------------|-----------------------|
| **Gene Defect**      | *WAS* (X-linked)                | *CYBB* (X-linked), *NCF1*, *NCF2*, *NCF4* | *CD40LG*, *CD40*, *AID* | *C5-C9*, *Factor H*   |
| **Key Clinical Features** | Eczema, Thrombocytopenia (small platelets), Recurrent Infections, Autoimmunity, Malignancy | Recurrent bacterial/fungal infections, Granulomas | Recurrent sinopulmonary infections, Opportunistic infections | Recurrent *Neisseria* infections |
| **Primary Immune Defect** | Actin cytoskeleton regulation, T-cell signaling, Platelet function | Phagocyte oxidative burst | Isotype switching, T-cell help | Opsonization, MAC formation |
| **Affected Cells**   | T cells, B cells, Platelets      | Phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages) | B cells, T cells     | Various (depending on component) |
| **Lab Findings**     | Thrombocytopenia (small platelets), T-cell lymphopenia, Low IgM | Normal platelet count, Normal lymphocyte counts | Normal platelet count, Normal lymphocyte counts, High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE | Normal platelet count, Normal lymphocyte counts |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene, leading to defective WASP protein function. WASP is critical for actin cytoskeleton organization and T-cell receptor signaling. This results in the classic triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections. Remember to differentiate WAS from other primary immunodeficiencies based on specific clinical features and laboratory findings (e.g., CGD involves granulomas and impaired oxidative burst; Hyper-IgM involves impaired isotype switching; Complement deficiencies predispose to *Neisseria* infections).

<a id='question-068'></a>

### Question 068: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 18 (ability)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular defect in Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS), a primary immunodeficiency characterized by eczema, thrombocytopenia, and recurrent infections, specifically focusing on the role of the WAS protein (WASP) in actin cytoskeleton rearrangement and immune cell function. (本题考察Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS）的分子缺陷，这是一种原发性免疫缺陷病，其特征是湿疹、血小板减少症和复发性感染，重点关注WASP蛋白在actin细胞骨架重排和免疫细胞功能中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the pediatrician with a history of severe eczema since infancy, frequent bleeding from minor cuts (suggesting thrombocytopenia), and recurrent respiratory infections, including pneumonia and otitis media. His mother reports that he has had multiple hospitalizations for these infections. Family history reveals that his maternal uncle died at a young age from complications of an unknown illness. Physical examination shows widespread eczematous lesions and petechiae on his lower extremities. A complete blood count (CBC) reveals thrombocytopenia (platelet count 25,000/µL) and small platelets. Immunoglobulin levels show low IgM and elevated IgA and IgE. Lymphocyte subset analysis shows a decreased number of T cells. Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *WAS* gene located on the X chromosome.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the immunodeficiency observed in this patient?

(A) Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the CD3 complex.
(B) Impaired actin cytoskeleton rearrangement due to loss of function of the WAS protein.
(C) Defective immunoglobulin class switching recombination due to mutations in the activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID) gene.
(D) Impaired phagocytosis and intracellular killing due to defects in the NADPH oxidase complex.
(E) Defective B-cell development due to mutations in the Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) gene.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **3-year-old boy:** Age is typical for presentation of primary immunodeficiencies.
- **Severe eczema, thrombocytopenia, recurrent respiratory infections:** Classic triad of Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS). Eczema is often severe and early-onset. Thrombocytopenia is characterized by low platelet count and small platelet size. Recurrent infections, particularly respiratory, are common due to T-cell dysfunction.
- **Family history (maternal uncle died young):** Suggests an X-linked inheritance pattern, consistent with WAS.
- **Physical exam (eczema, petechiae):** Confirms clinical findings suggestive of WAS.
- **CBC (thrombocytopenia, small platelets):** Characteristic hematologic finding in WAS.
- **Immunoglobulin levels (low IgM, high IgA/IgE):** Typical pattern in WAS, reflecting impaired T-cell help for B-cell responses, particularly for IgM production, and dysregulation of Ig class switching.
- **Lymphocyte subset analysis (decreased T cells):** Indicates T-cell deficiency, contributing to susceptibility to infections.
- **Genetic testing (*WAS* gene mutation):** Confirms the diagnosis of WAS.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying molecular mechanism of the immunodeficiency.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS):** An X-linked recessive primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene, which encodes the Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome protein (WASP).
- **WASP Function:** WASP is a cytoplasmic protein crucial for actin cytoskeleton organization and signaling in hematopoietic cells, particularly lymphocytes and platelets. It interacts with various signaling molecules and actin-binding proteins (like Arp2/3 complex, N-WASP, WAVE/Scar proteins) to regulate actin polymerization, cell motility, adhesion, and synapse formation.
- **Molecular Mechanism:** In immune cells, WASP is essential for T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling, B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling, and chemotaxis. It facilitates the formation of immunological synapses between T cells and antigen-presenting cells (APCs) and between cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and target cells. It also plays a role in platelet formation and function. Loss of WASP function leads to defective actin dynamics, impaired cell migration, defective signaling through TCR/BCR, reduced T-cell proliferation and cytokine production, impaired antibody responses (especially IgM), and thrombocytopenia with small platelets.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** The triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections (due to combined T-cell and B-cell defects) is characteristic. Patients are susceptible to opportunistic infections, autoimmune disorders, and malignancies.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective TCR signaling due to mutations in the CD3 complex:** Mutations in the CD3 complex (e.g., gamma chain) cause severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) with absent T cells and impaired NK cell function. While TCR signaling is affected in WAS, the primary defect is not in the CD3 complex itself but in the downstream actin regulation mediated by WASP. This option describes a different primary immunodeficiency.
- (C) **Defective immunoglobulin class switching recombination due to mutations in the activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID) gene:** Mutations in *AID* cause hyper-IgM syndrome type 2, characterized by normal or elevated IgM levels but severely reduced IgG, IgA, and IgE. This is due to impaired somatic hypermutation and class switch recombination. The immunoglobulin profile in WAS (low IgM, high IgA/IgE) is different.
- (D) **Impaired phagocytosis and intracellular killing due to defects in the NADPH oxidase complex:** Defects in the NADPH oxidase complex cause Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD), characterized by recurrent bacterial and fungal infections, particularly catalase-positive organisms, and granuloma formation. This involves phagocytes, not primarily lymphocytes or platelets, and the defect is in oxidative burst, not actin cytoskeleton rearrangement.
- (E) **Defective B-cell development due to mutations in the Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) gene:** Mutations in *BTK* cause X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), characterized by a lack of mature B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia (very low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes). T-cell function is normal in XLA. The clinical presentation (eczema, thrombocytopenia, T-cell defects) and immunoglobulin profile (low IgM, high IgA/IgE) are inconsistent with XLA.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS) | SCID (CD3 defect) | Hyper-IgM Syndrome Type 2 (AID defect) | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :---------------- | :------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| **Gene Defect**         | *WAS*                          | *CD3G*, etc.      | *AID*                                  | *CYBB*, etc.                        | *BTK*                             |
| **Inheritance**         | X-linked                       | Autosomal Recessive | Autosomal Recessive                    | X-linked or Autosomal Recessive      | X-linked                          |
| **Key Clinical Features** | Eczema, Thrombocytopenia (small platelets), Recurrent Infections | Severe Infections (early), Failure to Thrive | Recurrent Infections, Normal/High IgM | Recurrent Bacterial/Fungal Infections, Granulomas | Recurrent Bacterial Infections |
| **Primary Cell Type Affected** | Lymphocytes, Platelets        | T cells, NK cells | B cells                                | Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages) | B cells                           |
| **Immunoglobulin Profile** | Low IgM, High IgA/IgE          | Normal/Low (if maternal IgG wanes) | Normal/High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE      | Normal                               | Panhypogammaglobulinemia          |
| **Molecular Defect**    | Actin cytoskeleton rearrangement | TCR signaling     | Class Switch Recombination             | NADPH Oxidase Function              | B-cell development                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene, leading to a defective WASP protein. WASP is critical for actin cytoskeleton organization in hematopoietic cells. The resulting defect impairs T-cell and B-cell function (especially IgM production) and platelet formation/function, leading to the characteristic triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections. Differentiate WAS from other primary immunodeficiencies based on the specific clinical triad, immunoglobulin profile, and affected cell types (e.g., XLA affects only B cells, CGD affects phagocytes, Hyper-IgM affects class switching).

<a id='question-069'></a>

### Question 069: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 19 (convert)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Defective intracellular trafficking of immunoglobulins due to mutations in the heavy chain transporter protein (TAP1/TAP2) leads to impaired antigen presentation and recurrent infections, particularly with intracellular pathogens. (缺陷的免疫球蛋白细胞内转运，由重链转运蛋白（TAP1/TAP2）突变引起，导致抗原呈递受损和复发性感染，特别是与细胞内病原体相关的感染。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent episodes of pneumonia, otitis media, and chronic diarrhea since infancy. He has failed to thrive and exhibits persistent candidiasis of the oral mucosa. His immunizations were completed according to schedule, but he did not develop protective antibody titers against vaccine-preventable diseases like *Haemophilus influenzae* type b (Hib) and *Streptococcus pneumoniae*. Physical examination reveals a pale, cachectic child with hepatosplenomegaly. Laboratory investigations show normal serum immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) and normal B-cell and T-cell counts. However, his T-cells exhibit markedly reduced cytotoxic activity against virally infected cells and tumor cells in *in vitro* assays. Flow cytometry reveals normal expression of CD4 and CD8 on T-cells, but intracellular staining for MHC class I molecules is significantly decreased on peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs). Further genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *TAP1* gene.

Which of the following mechanisms best explains the impaired cytotoxic T-lymphocyte (CTL) function and lack of antibody response to polysaccharide antigens in this patient?

(A) Defective phagosome-lysosome fusion leading to impaired antigen processing by macrophages and dendritic cells.
(B) Impaired transport of peptides from the cytoplasm into the endoplasmic reticulum, preventing loading onto MHC class I molecules.
(C) Defective synthesis of MHC class II molecules, leading to impaired antigen presentation to CD4+ T-cells.
(D) Defective B-cell maturation and immunoglobulin class switching due to mutations in activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID).
(E) Defective complement activation pathway leading to impaired opsonization and bacterial killing.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent infections (pneumonia, otitis media, diarrhea, candidiasis):** Suggests an underlying immunodeficiency. The types of infections (bacterial, fungal, viral implied by lack of response to vaccines) point towards a defect in cell-mediated immunity and/or antibody production.
- **Failure to thrive, hepatosplenomegaly:** Common signs of chronic infection and inflammation in immunodeficient patients.
- **Lack of antibody response to polysaccharide antigens (Hib, Pneumococcus):** Indicates a defect in T-cell dependent B-cell activation or antigen presentation, as polysaccharide antigens are typically T-independent, but conjugate vaccines require T-cell help for robust memory B-cell formation. The lack of response suggests a T-cell defect impacting B-cell help.
- **Normal serum immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) and normal B/T cell counts:** Rules out common variable immunodeficiency (CVID) which typically has low Ig levels, and severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) which has low lymphocyte counts. This points towards a functional defect rather than a numerical deficiency or global B-cell defect.
- **Markedly reduced cytotoxic activity of T-cells:** Indicates a defect in CTL function, which relies on MHC class I presentation of intracellular antigens.
- **Decreased MHC class I expression on PBMCs:** Directly points to a problem with the MHC class I pathway.
- **Homozygous mutation in the *TAP1* gene:** This is the definitive clue. TAP1 (Transporter associated with Antigen Processing 1) and TAP2 form the TAP complex, which transports peptides from the cytosol into the endoplasmic reticulum (ER) for loading onto MHC class I molecules. A mutation here directly impairs this process.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **MHC Class I Antigen Presentation Pathway:** Intracellular proteins (viral, tumor, self) are degraded by the proteasome into peptides. These peptides are transported into the ER by the TAP complex (TAP1/TAP2). In the ER, peptides bind to newly synthesized MHC class I heavy chains, stabilized by chaperones like calnexin and tapasin. The stable MHC class I-peptide complex is then transported to the cell surface for presentation to CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).
- **Role of TAP:** The TAP complex is essential for transporting peptides into the ER lumen. Without functional TAP, peptides cannot access MHC class I molecules, leading to unstable MHC class I molecules that are rapidly degraded or retained in the ER. This results in decreased surface expression of MHC class I molecules and impaired presentation of intracellular antigens to CTLs.
- **Clinical Manifestations of TAP Deficiency (Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome Type 1):** Patients present with recurrent infections, particularly with intracellular pathogens like *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*, *Listeria monocytogenes*, and viruses. They also have an increased risk of developing squamous cell carcinomas of the skin, likely due to impaired immune surveillance against tumor antigens presented via MHC class I. The lack of CTL function explains the susceptibility to intracellular pathogens. The lack of T-cell help for B-cells explains the poor response to polysaccharide antigens (even though total Ig levels might be normal initially, specific antibody responses are impaired).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Defective phagosome-lysosome fusion (e.g., Chediak-Higashi syndrome) impairs antigen processing by phagocytes (macrophages, dendritic cells) but primarily affects MHC class II presentation and phagocytic killing. It doesn't directly explain the decreased MHC class I expression or impaired CTL function seen here.
- (C) Defective synthesis of MHC class II molecules (e.g., MHC class II deficiency) impairs antigen presentation to CD4+ T-cells, leading to defects in T-helper cell function and antibody responses. However, MHC class I expression and CTL function would be normal, which contradicts the findings in the vignette.
- (D) Defective B-cell maturation and immunoglobulin class switching (e.g., Hyper-IgM syndrome due to CD40L deficiency or AID deficiency) primarily affects antibody production and isotype switching. While it can lead to recurrent infections, T-cell counts and function (including CTL activity and MHC class I expression) are typically normal. The vignette specifically points to a CTL defect and decreased MHC class I.
- (E) Defective complement activation pathway (e.g., C3 deficiency) impairs opsonization, inflammation, and direct lysis of pathogens. This leads to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria but does not directly explain the impaired CTL function or decreased MHC class I expression.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | TAP1/TAP2 Deficiency (Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome Type 1) | Chediak-Higashi Syndrome | MHC Class II Deficiency | Hyper-IgM Syndrome (AID Defect) | Complement Deficiency (C3) |
| :-------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------ | :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**          | TAP complex function (MHC I peptide transport)          | Phagosome-lysosome fusion | MHC Class II synthesis  | B-cell class switch recombination | Complement component defect |
| **MHC Class I Expression**  | Decreased                                              | Normal                    | Normal                  | Normal                         | Normal                     |
| **MHC Class II Expression** | Normal                                                 | Normal                    | Decreased               | Normal                         | Normal                     |
| **CTL Function**            | Impaired                                               | Normal                    | Normal                  | Normal                         | Normal                     |
| **CD4+ T-cell Function**    | Normal                                                 | Normal                    | Impaired                | Normal                         | Normal                     |
| **B-cell Function**         | Impaired T-dependent responses                          | Normal                    | Impaired                | Impaired isotype switching     | Normal                     |
| **Key Infections**          | Intracellular pathogens, SCC                           | Pyogenic bacteria        | Pyogenic bacteria       | Opportunistic infections        | Encapsulated bacteria      |
| **Genetic Basis**           | TAP1/TAP2 mutations                                     | LYST mutation             | HLA-DR, DQ, DP mutations | AID mutation                   | Various complement genes   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The vignette describes a patient with recurrent infections, impaired CTL function, decreased MHC class I expression, and a mutation in *TAP1*. This points to a defect in the intracellular pathway responsible for loading peptides onto MHC class I molecules. The TAP complex (TAP1/TAP2) is crucial for this process. Defective TAP function leads to Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome Type 1, characterized by impaired CTL responses and increased susceptibility to intracellular pathogens and certain cancers. Differentiating this from other primary immunodeficiencies requires understanding the specific pathways affected (MHC I vs. MHC II, B-cell maturation, phagocytosis, complement).

<a id='question-070'></a>

### Question 070: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 20 (hydrogen)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Defective NADPH oxidase complex leads to impaired oxidative burst in phagocytes, resulting in recurrent bacterial and fungal infections, particularly catalase-positive organisms, and granuloma formation. (NADPH 氧化酶复合体缺陷导致吞噬细胞氧化爆发受损，引起复发性细菌和真菌感染，特别是催化酶阳性微生物，并形成肉芽肿。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents with a history of recurrent, severe bacterial infections starting in infancy, including pneumonia, skin abscesses, and lymphadenitis. His infections are often caused by *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, and *Aspergillus* species. He has also developed chronic granulomatous disease, evidenced by granulomas in the lungs, lymph nodes, and gastrointestinal tract. Laboratory studies show normal serum immunoglobulin levels and normal lymphocyte counts. A dihydrorhodamine (DHR) flow cytometry assay reveals absent oxidative burst in neutrophils upon stimulation with phorbol myristate acetate (PMA). His maternal grandmother had a similar history of recurrent infections.

Which of the following molecular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells
(B) Defective NADPH oxidase subunits
(C) Defective Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) signaling
(D) Defective complement component C3
(E) Defective adenosine deaminase (ADA) enzyme activity

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe bacterial and fungal infections:** Suggests a defect in phagocytic function or cell-mediated immunity. The specific organisms (*S. aureus*, *Serratia*, *Burkholderia*, *Aspergillus*) are catalase-positive, which is a key clue. Catalase breaks down hydrogen peroxide, which is crucial for killing by phagocytes.
- **Chronic granulomatous disease (CGD):** Indicates impaired killing of ingested microbes by phagocytes, leading to persistent inflammation and granuloma formation.
- **Normal serum immunoglobulin levels and lymphocyte counts:** Rules out humoral immunodeficiencies (like XLA, CVID) and severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) affecting lymphocyte development.
- **Dihydrorhodamine (DHR) flow cytometry assay reveals absent oxidative burst:** This is the diagnostic hallmark of CGD. Neutrophils normally produce reactive oxygen species (ROS) via the NADPH oxidase complex during the respiratory burst to kill microbes. Absence of DHR oxidation indicates a defect in this process.
- **Maternal grandmother had similar history:** Suggests an X-linked inheritance pattern, common for some forms of CGD.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **NADPH Oxidase Complex:** This multi-subunit enzyme complex is located in the phagosomal membrane of neutrophils and macrophages. Its function is to generate superoxide radicals (O₂⁻) by transferring electrons from NADPH to molecular oxygen (O₂). This is the first step in the respiratory burst.
- **Respiratory Burst:** Superoxide radicals are rapidly converted to other ROS, including hydrogen peroxide (H₂O₂), hydroxyl radicals (•OH), and hypochlorous acid (HOCl, via myeloperoxidase). These ROS are highly toxic to ingested microbes.
- **Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD):** Caused by genetic defects in any of the five subunits of the NADPH oxidase complex (gp91phox, p22phox, p47phox, p67phox, p40phox). The most common form (about 70% of cases) is X-linked, caused by mutations in the *CYBB* gene encoding the gp91phox subunit. Other forms are autosomal recessive.
- **Pathogenesis:** Without a functional NADPH oxidase, phagocytes cannot produce ROS. This impairs their ability to kill catalase-positive bacteria and fungi, which can break down the H₂O₂ produced by the phagocyte. The inability to clear these organisms leads to chronic inflammation and granuloma formation.
- **DHR Assay:** DHR is a non-fluorescent dye that is oxidized to a fluorescent product (rhodamine 123) by ROS produced during the respiratory burst. In CGD, neutrophils fail to oxidize DHR upon stimulation, indicating a defective respiratory burst.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells:** This causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal or elevated IgM levels but low IgG, IgA, and IgE. They are susceptible to opportunistic infections, particularly *Pneumocystis jirovecii*. This does not fit the clinical picture of recurrent bacterial/fungal infections with catalase-positive organisms and absent oxidative burst.
- (C) **Defective Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) signaling:** JAK3 is crucial for signaling downstream of several cytokine receptors (e.g., IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, IL-21). Defects in JAK3 cause X-linked SCID, characterized by severe lymphopenia (T-, B-, NK- cells) and susceptibility to a wide range of infections from infancy. This patient has normal lymphocyte counts and immunoglobulin levels.
- (D) **Defective complement component C3:** C3 deficiency (or deficiency of other early complement components like C1q, C2, C4) leads to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*, *Neisseria meningitidis*) and autoimmune diseases (like SLE). It does not typically cause recurrent fungal infections or granulomas, nor does it affect the oxidative burst.
- (E) **Defective adenosine deaminase (ADA) enzyme activity:** ADA deficiency is a cause of autosomal recessive SCID. ADA is involved in purine metabolism. Its deficiency leads to the accumulation of toxic metabolites (deoxyadenosine) in lymphocytes, causing their death. This results in severe lymphopenia and susceptibility to opportunistic infections, similar to JAK3 deficiency. This patient has normal lymphocyte counts.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | X-linked Hyper-IgM Syndrome | X-linked SCID (JAK3 Def.) | C3 Deficiency | ADA Deficiency (SCID) |
|-------------------------|--------------------------------------|-----------------------------|---------------------------|---------------|-----------------------|
| **Primary Defect**      | NADPH Oxidase Complex                | CD40L on T cells            | JAK3 Signaling            | C3 Protein    | ADA Enzyme            |
| **Immune Cell Affected**| Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages)| T cells                     | T, B, NK cells            | N/A           | T, B, NK cells        |
| **Key Lab Finding**     | Absent Oxidative Burst (DHR assay)   | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE   | Severe Lymphopenia        | Low C3        | Severe Lymphopenia    |
| **Typical Infections**  | Catalase+ Bacteria/Fungi (*Staph*, *Serratia*, *Aspergillus*) | *Pneumocystis*, Opportunistic | Opportunistic (Viral, Fungal, Bacterial) | Encapsulated Bacteria | Opportunistic (Viral, Fungal, Bacterial) |
| **Granulomas?**         | Yes                                  | No                          | No                        | No            | No                    |
| **Inheritance**         | X-linked (most common), AR           | X-linked                    | X-linked                  | AR/AD         | AR                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) is a primary immunodeficiency characterized by defective phagocyte oxidative burst due to mutations in NADPH oxidase subunits. Key features include recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms and granuloma formation. The DHR assay is diagnostic. Differentiate CGD from other primary immunodeficiencies affecting phagocytes (e.g., Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency) or lymphocytes (e.g., SCID, Hyper-IgM syndrome) based on specific clinical features, lab findings, and affected pathways.

<a id='question-071'></a>

### Question 071: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 21 (peroxide)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) is caused by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex, leading to impaired phagocyte oxidative burst and inability to kill catalase-positive organisms. (慢性肉芽肿病 (CGD) 是由 NADPH 氧化酶复合物缺陷引起的，导致吞噬细胞氧化爆发受损，无法杀死 catalase 阳性微生物。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 2-week history of fever, cough, and shortness of breath. He has a history of recurrent pneumonia, skin abscesses, and lymphadenitis since infancy, often requiring hospitalization. His mother reports that his infections are typically caused by bacteria like *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Serratia marcescens*. Family history is significant for a similar presentation in his maternal grandfather. Physical examination reveals a cachectic boy with bilateral crackles in the lungs and multiple draining skin lesions on his extremities. Laboratory studies show leukocytosis with neutrophilia. A chest X-ray confirms bilateral pneumonia with multiple cavitary lesions. Sputum culture grows *Aspergillus fumigatus*. A neutrophil function test, specifically the dihydrorhodamine (DHR) flow cytometry assay, shows absent oxidative burst upon stimulation with phorbol myristate acetate (PMA).

Which of the following intracellular pathways is most likely defective in this patient?

(A) Lysosomal trafficking pathway involving the lysosomal-associated membrane protein (LAMP)
(B) Pathway responsible for the production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) by phagocytes
(C) Pathway involved in the synthesis of mannose-6-phosphate (M6P) receptors
(D) Pathway responsible for the secretion of antimicrobial peptides like defensins
(E) Pathway involved in the processing and presentation of exogenous antigens via MHC class II molecules

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 6-year-old boy with recurrent, severe infections (pneumonia, skin abscesses, lymphadenitis) since infancy. This pattern suggests a primary immunodeficiency affecting innate immunity, particularly phagocytes.
- **Infecting Organisms:** *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Serratia marcescens* are catalase-positive bacteria. *Aspergillus fumigatus* is a catalase-positive fungus. Infections with catalase-positive organisms are characteristic of defects in the oxidative burst.
- **Family History:** Similar presentation in maternal grandfather suggests an X-linked inheritance pattern, common for CGD.
- **Physical Exam:** Cachexia, lung crackles, draining skin lesions are consistent with severe, chronic infections.
- **Diagnostic Test:** Dihydrorhodamine (DHR) flow cytometry assay shows absent oxidative burst upon PMA stimulation. This is the gold standard test for diagnosing CGD, directly demonstrating the functional defect in phagocyte ROS production.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the defective intracellular pathway. Based on the clinical picture and DHR test result, the defect lies in the phagocyte's ability to produce ROS.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD):** This is a primary immunodeficiency characterized by defects in the NADPH oxidase enzyme complex.
- **NADPH Oxidase Function:** This multi-subunit enzyme complex (gp91phox, p22phox, p47phox, p67phox, p40phox) is located in the phagosomal membrane of neutrophils, macrophages, and monocytes. Upon activation (e.g., by PMA or microbial products), its subunits assemble to catalyze the reduction of molecular oxygen (O2) to superoxide anion (O2•−).
- **Oxidative Burst:** Superoxide anion is rapidly converted to other ROS, including hydrogen peroxide (H2O2), hydroxyl radical (•OH), and hypochlorous acid (HOCl, via myeloperoxidase). These ROS are crucial for killing ingested microbes, especially catalase-positive organisms. Catalase breaks down H2O2 into water and oxygen, preventing the microbe from being killed by H2O2. Therefore, phagocytes lacking ROS production cannot effectively kill catalase-positive pathogens.
- **Pathophysiology:** The defect in NADPH oxidase prevents the generation of ROS, leading to impaired microbial killing within phagolysosomes. This results in the formation of granulomas (collections of activated macrophages and lymphocytes) as the immune system attempts to wall off the persistent infection.
- **Inheritance:** Most cases (65-70%) are X-linked recessive (mutation in *CYBB* gene encoding gp91phox). Autosomal recessive forms exist (mutations in *CYBA*, *NCF1*, *NCF2*, *NCF4* genes encoding p22phox, p47phox, p67phox, p40phox, respectively).
- **DHR Assay:** Dihydrorhodamine is a non-fluorescent dye that is oxidized by ROS to fluorescent rhodamine. In normal neutrophils, PMA stimulation triggers the oxidative burst, oxidizing DHR and causing a significant increase in fluorescence. In CGD neutrophils, the lack of ROS production prevents DHR oxidation, resulting in minimal or absent fluorescence.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Lysosomal trafficking pathway involving LAMP:** Defects in lysosomal trafficking, such as Chediak-Higashi syndrome (mutation in *LYST* gene), lead to impaired fusion of lysosomes with phagosomes, resulting in giant granules and susceptibility to pyogenic infections. This does not directly explain the failure to kill catalase-positive organisms or the absent oxidative burst.
- (C) **Pathway involved in the synthesis of mannose-6-phosphate (M6P) receptors:** Defects in M6P receptors (e.g., in Mucolipidosis II or III) impair lysosomal enzyme trafficking, leading to lysosomal storage disorders. This is unrelated to phagocyte function and susceptibility to specific infections seen in CGD.
- (D) **Pathway responsible for the secretion of antimicrobial peptides like defensins:** Defensins are secreted by Paneth cells in the gut and other cells. While important for innate immunity, defects in their secretion are not the primary mechanism in CGD. CGD specifically affects the intracellular killing mechanism of phagocytes via ROS.
- (E) **Pathway involved in the processing and presentation of exogenous antigens via MHC class II molecules:** Defects in MHC class II processing or expression (e.g., Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome) impair antigen presentation to CD4+ T cells, leading to severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or susceptibility to specific infections, but not the characteristic pattern of catalase-positive infections and absent oxidative burst seen in CGD.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | Chediak-Higashi Syndrome | Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) | Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :------------------------ | :--------------------------------------- | :------------------------ |
| **Primary Defect**      | NADPH oxidase complex               | LYST gene (trafficking)   | Various (e.g., RAG1/2, JAK3, IL2RG)      | MHC class II genes        |
| **Cell Type Affected**  | Phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages) | Phagocytes, melanocytes   | Lymphocytes (T and B cells)              | Antigen-presenting cells |
| **Key Mechanism**       | Impaired oxidative burst (ROS)       | Impaired phagolysosome fusion, giant granules | Impaired lymphocyte development/function | Impaired antigen presentation |
| **Characteristic Infections** | Catalase-positive bacteria, fungi | Pyogenic bacteria (Staph, Strep) | Broad range (bacterial, viral, fungal, protozoal) | Opportunistic infections |
| **Diagnostic Test**     | DHR flow cytometry (absent burst)   | Giant granules on blood smear | Lymphopenia, absent T/B cell function   | Absent MHC class II expression |
| **Inheritance**         | X-linked (most common), AR           | AR                       | AR, X-linked                              | AR                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CGD is a defect in phagocyte oxidative burst due to NADPH oxidase deficiency, leading to susceptibility to catalase-positive organisms. The DHR assay is diagnostic. Differentiate CGD from other phagocyte defects like Chediak-Higashi (lysosomal trafficking) and other primary immunodeficiencies affecting lymphocytes (SCID) or antigen presentation (Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome).

<a id='question-072'></a>

### Question 072: Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) is a primary immunodeficiency characterized by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex, leading to impaired phagocyte oxidative burst and susceptibility to catalase-positive organisms. (慢性肉芽肿病是一种原发性免疫缺陷病，其特征是 NADPH 氧化酶复合物的缺陷，导致吞噬细胞的氧化爆发受损，并易感于 catalase 阳性微生物。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 2-week history of recurrent, severe bacterial and fungal infections, including pneumonia, skin abscesses, and lymphadenitis. His mother reports that these infections are unusually difficult to treat and often recur despite appropriate antibiotic therapy. Family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died at age 10 from overwhelming sepsis. Physical examination reveals multiple draining skin lesions, hepatosplenomegaly, and cervical lymphadenopathy. Laboratory studies show leukocytosis with neutrophilia. A skin biopsy from an abscess reveals numerous neutrophils surrounding necrotic tissue, but these neutrophils lack the ability to kill ingested bacteria. A dihydrorhodamine (DHR) flow cytometry assay demonstrates absent oxidative burst in neutrophils upon stimulation with phorbol myristate acetate (PMA). Which of the following molecular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Defective synthesis of the common gamma chain (γc) shared by IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21 receptors.
(B) Defective expression of CD40 ligand (CD40L) on activated T cells.
(C) Defective assembly or function of the NADPH oxidase complex.
(D) Defective expression of the CD154 protein on T cells, leading to impaired B cell class switching.
(E) Defective expression of the enzyme adenosine deaminase (ADA), leading to the accumulation of toxic metabolites in lymphocytes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 5-year-old boy with recurrent, severe bacterial and fungal infections (pneumonia, skin abscesses, lymphadenitis) that are difficult to treat. This pattern suggests a defect in phagocytic function or cell-mediated immunity.
- **Family History:** Maternal uncle died young from sepsis, suggesting a possible X-linked inheritance pattern.
- **Physical Exam:** Multiple draining skin lesions, hepatosplenomegaly, lymphadenopathy are consistent with chronic infection and inflammation.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Leukocytosis with neutrophilia indicates an active inflammatory response, but the inability to clear infections points to a functional defect in neutrophils.
- **Histopathology:** Neutrophils present at the site of infection but unable to kill bacteria strongly implicates a defect in the neutrophil killing mechanism, specifically the oxidative burst.
- **Diagnostic Test:** Dihydrorhodamine (DHR) flow cytometry shows absent oxidative burst upon PMA stimulation. This is the gold standard test for diagnosing Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). PMA activates protein kinase C, which phosphorylates and activates components of the NADPH oxidase complex. The absence of DHR oxidation confirms a defect in this pathway.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying molecular defect responsible for the patient's condition, which is CGD.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD):** CGD is a primary immunodeficiency affecting phagocytes (neutrophils, monocytes, macrophages). It is caused by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex, a multi-subunit enzyme complex located in the phagosomal membrane.
- **NADPH Oxidase Function:** This complex is responsible for generating superoxide radicals (O2-) during the respiratory burst, which is crucial for killing ingested pathogens, particularly catalase-positive bacteria and fungi. Superoxide is converted to other reactive oxygen species (ROS) like hydrogen peroxide (H2O2) and hypochlorous acid (HOCl, bleach), which are highly toxic to microbes.
- **Molecular Basis:** Mutations in any of the five subunits of the NADPH oxidase complex (gp91phox/NOX2, p22phox/CYBB, p47phox/NCF1, p67phox/NCF2, p40phox/NCF4) can cause CGD. The most common form (~70%) is X-linked, caused by mutations in the *CYBB* gene encoding p22phox (gp91phox). Autosomal recessive forms result from mutations in the other subunits.
- **Pathophysiology:** Without a functional NADPH oxidase, phagocytes cannot produce ROS. This leads to impaired killing of catalase-positive organisms (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, *Aspergillus* species) because these organisms produce catalase, which breaks down H2O2, preventing its accumulation to toxic levels. The inability to clear these pathogens results in recurrent infections and the formation of granulomas (collections of activated macrophages and lymphocytes) in various tissues, hence the name "Chronic Granulomatous Disease".
- **DHR Assay:** Dihydrorhodamine (DHR) is a non-fluorescent dye that is oxidized to fluorescent rhodamine by ROS produced during the oxidative burst. In CGD patients, neutrophils fail to oxidize DHR upon stimulation, confirming the defect.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective synthesis of the common gamma chain (γc) shared by IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21 receptors.** This describes X-linked Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (X-SCID). Patients with X-SCID have profound defects in T cell and NK cell development, leading to severe susceptibility to opportunistic infections early in life. This patient has neutrophilia and granulomas, not the typical presentation of SCID (lymphopenia, failure to thrive, opportunistic infections).
- (B) **Defective expression of CD40 ligand (CD40L) on activated T cells.** This describes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. CD40L on T cells interacts with CD40 on B cells, macrophages, and dendritic cells. Defective CD40L impairs T cell-dependent B cell activation, isotype switching (leading to high IgM and low IgG, IgA, IgE), and macrophage activation. Patients typically present with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, opportunistic infections (like *Pneumocystis jirovecii*), and sometimes neutropenia. This does not fit the patient's presentation of recurrent bacterial/fungal abscesses and granulomas with neutrophilia.
- (D) **Defective expression of the CD154 protein on T cells, leading to impaired B cell class switching.** CD154 is another name for CD40L. Therefore, this option is identical to option (B) and describes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome, which is incorrect for the reasons stated above.
- (E) **Defective expression of the enzyme adenosine deaminase (ADA), leading to the accumulation of toxic metabolites in lymphocytes.** This describes ADA deficiency, a form of Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID). ADA deficiency leads to the accumulation of deoxyadenosine and deoxy-ATP, which are toxic to lymphocytes, particularly T and B cells. Patients present similarly to X-SCID with severe infections and lymphopenia. This does not fit the patient's presentation of granulomas, neutrophilia, and a specific defect in phagocyte oxidative burst.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | X-linked SCID (γc deficiency) | X-linked Hyper-IgM (CD40L deficiency) | ADA Deficiency (SCID) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | NADPH oxidase complex               | Common γ chain (IL receptors) | CD40 Ligand (CD154)                  | Adenosine Deaminase |
| **Affected Cells**      | Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages) | T cells, NK cells            | T cells, B cells, Macrophages        | T cells, B cells     |
| **Key Immunological Defect** | Impaired oxidative burst            | T cell & NK cell development | Impaired B cell class switching, Macrophage activation | Lymphocyte toxicity |
| **Typical Infections**  | Catalase-positive bacteria/fungi (Staph, Serratia, Burkholderia, Aspergillus) | Opportunistic (PCP, CMV, Candida) | Opportunistic (PCP, CMV), Sinopulmonary bacterial | Opportunistic (PCP, CMV, Candida) |
| **Granulomas?**         | Yes                                 | No                           | No                                   | No                  |
| **Neutrophilia?**       | Yes                                 | No (Lymphopenia)             | No (Neutropenia possible)            | No (Lymphopenia)     |
| **Inheritance**         | X-linked (most common), AR          | X-linked                    | X-linked                             | Autosomal Recessive  |
| **Diagnostic Test**     | DHR flow cytometry                  | T cell enumeration          | IgM levels, CD40L expression          | ADA enzyme assay     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CGD is characterized by defective phagocyte oxidative burst due to NADPH oxidase mutations, leading to recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms and granuloma formation. DHR flow cytometry is diagnostic. Key differentials include other primary immunodeficiencies affecting phagocytes (e.g., Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency) or T cells (e.g., SCID, Hyper-IgM syndrome), which present differently (e.g., lack of pus formation, severe lymphopenia, specific immunoglobulin abnormalities).

<a id='question-073'></a>

### Question 073: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 23 (Defects)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Defects in intracellular signaling pathways, specifically those involving tyrosine kinases (e.g., JAKs, SYK), lead to impaired immune cell function and increased susceptibility to specific infections. (细胞内信号通路缺陷，特别是酪氨酸激酶（如JAKs, SYK）缺陷，导致免疫细胞功能障碍和易感性增加)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia requiring hospitalization twice in the past year and chronic otitis media. He has a history of failure to thrive and eczema. Family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died at age 10 from complications of an unknown immunodeficiency. Physical examination reveals a pale, cachectic child with hepatosplenomegaly. Laboratory studies show markedly elevated serum IgE levels (>5000 IU/mL), eosinophilia, and normal lymphocyte counts. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals absent expression of CD3ζ chain on T cells and NK cells. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *TYK2* gene.

Which of the following intracellular signaling pathways is most directly impaired in this patient?

(A) B cell receptor (BCR) signaling pathway
(B) T cell receptor (TCR) signaling pathway
(C) Complement activation pathway
(D) Toll-like receptor (TLR) signaling pathway
(E) Interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) signaling pathway

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections, failure to thrive, eczema:** Suggests an underlying immunodeficiency, potentially involving T cells, B cells, or phagocytes. The constellation is common in primary immunodeficiencies.
- **Family history of early death from immunodeficiency:** Points towards a genetic basis for the condition.
- **Hepatosplenomegaly:** Can be seen in various immunodeficiencies, often due to chronic infections or autoimmune phenomena.
- **Elevated serum IgE, eosinophilia:** Suggests dysregulation of Th2 responses or impaired negative regulation.
- **Absent CD3ζ chain expression:** This is a critical clue. CD3ζ is a key component of the T cell receptor (TCR) complex and the natural killer (NK) cell activating receptor complex. Its absence disrupts signaling downstream of these receptors.
- ***TYK2* gene mutation:** This gene encodes Tyrosine Kinase 2, a Janus kinase (JAK) family member. TYK2 is crucial for signaling downstream of several cytokine receptors, including those for type I interferons (IFN-α/β), IL-12, IL-15, IL-21, and IL-23.
- **Putting it together:** The clinical picture (recurrent infections, eczema, elevated IgE), the specific molecular defect (absent CD3ζ, *TYK2* mutation), and the immunological findings (impaired T/NK cell signaling) point towards a specific primary immunodeficiency. The *TYK2* mutation directly impacts the IFN-γ signaling pathway, which is critical for macrophage activation and defense against intracellular pathogens. While CD3ζ absence affects TCR signaling, the *TYK2* mutation specifically targets the IFN-γ pathway.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **TYK2 (Tyrosine Kinase 2):** A non-receptor tyrosine kinase belonging to the JAK family. It plays a vital role in intracellular signaling downstream of various cytokine receptors.
- **JAK-STAT Pathway:** Cytokine binding to its receptor leads to receptor-associated JAKs (like TYK2) phosphorylating each other and the receptor. This creates docking sites for STAT proteins (Signal Transducers and Activators of Transcription). JAKs then phosphorylate STATs, which dimerize, translocate to the nucleus, and regulate gene transcription.
- **IFN-γ Signaling:** IFN-γ binds to its receptor (IFNGR), which associates with TYK2 and JAK1/JAK2. Activation of TYK2 is essential for initiating the JAK-STAT pathway leading to the expression of genes involved in macrophage activation, antigen presentation, and Th1 differentiation.
- **CD3ζ Chain:** An adaptor protein crucial for TCR signaling. It contains immunoreceptor tyrosine-based activation motifs (ITAMs). Upon TCR engagement, Src family kinases phosphorylate ITAMs, recruiting ZAP-70 kinase, which then phosphorylates downstream targets, leading to T cell activation. Absence of CD3ζ severely impairs TCR signaling.
- **Clinical Manifestations of TYK2 Deficiency:** Patients typically present with severe, early-onset viral infections (especially enteroviruses and varicella-zoster virus), susceptibility to mycobacterial infections, and impaired responses to live vaccines. The elevated IgE and eczema are also characteristic features. The absence of CD3ζ is a separate finding, potentially coincidental or related to a different underlying defect, but the *TYK2* mutation is the primary driver of the immunodeficiency described. The question asks about the pathway *most directly* impaired by the *TYK2* mutation.
- **IFN-γ Pathway Importance:** IFN-γ is critical for activating macrophages to kill intracellular pathogens like *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*, *Listeria monocytogenes*, and certain viruses. Impaired IFN-γ signaling leads to increased susceptibility to these infections.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **B cell receptor (BCR) signaling pathway:** BCR signaling is crucial for B cell activation, proliferation, and antibody production. While B cell function can be indirectly affected in some immunodeficiencies, the primary defect in TYK2 deficiency is not in the BCR pathway itself. BCR signaling involves different kinases like Syk and BTK.
- (B) **T cell receptor (TCR) signaling pathway:** TCR signaling is essential for T cell activation. While the patient has absent CD3ζ, which directly impairs TCR signaling, the question stem highlights the *TYK2* mutation. TYK2 is not a primary component of the TCR signaling complex itself, although TCR signaling can influence cytokine production that relies on TYK2. The *TYK2* mutation's most direct impact is on cytokine receptor signaling, particularly IFN-γ.
- (C) **Complement activation pathway:** The complement system is part of the innate immune system, involving a cascade of proteins leading to opsonization, inflammation, and cell lysis. Defects in complement components lead to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria. TYK2 is not directly involved in the complement cascade.
- (D) **Toll-like receptor (TLR) signaling pathway:** TLRs are pattern recognition receptors that initiate innate immune responses upon recognition of microbial components. TLR signaling involves adaptor proteins like MyD88 and TRIF, and kinases like IRAK and TBK1. TYK2 is not a core component of TLR signaling pathways.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | TYK2 Deficiency (JAK1/JAK2 Deficiency) | CD3ζ Deficiency | BCR Signaling Defect (e.g., XLA) | TCR Signaling Defect (e.g., SCID) | TLR Signaling Defect (e.g., IRF3/IRF7 Def.) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :-------------- | :------------------------------ | :-------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | *TYK2* gene mutation                    | CD3ζ absence    | *BTK* gene mutation             | Various (e.g., *RAG1/2*, *CD3D/E/G*) | *IRF3/7* gene mutation                   |
| **Key Pathway Affected** | IFN-γ signaling (JAK-STAT)               | TCR signaling   | BCR signaling                  | TCR signaling                    | TLR signaling                            |
| **Cytokine Receptor**   | IFN-γR, IL-12R, IL-15R, IL-21R, IL-23R  | N/A             | N/A                             | N/A                               | N/A                                      |
| **Key Kinase**          | TYK2 (JAK family)                       | ZAP-70, Lck     | Syk, BTK                        | Lck, ZAP-70                       | IRAK, TBK1                               |
| **Typical Infections**  | Viral (Enterovirus, VZV), Mycobacteria | Variable       | Encapsulated bacteria           | Broad (Viral, Bacterial, Fungal)   | Viral (HSV, VZV)                         |
| **Other Features**      | Elevated IgE, Eczema                    | Variable       | Absent B cells, Agammaglobulinemia | Absent T cells                    | Variable                                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Takeaway:** Defects in JAK-STAT pathways, specifically involving TYK2, impair signaling downstream of key cytokines like IFN-γ, leading to increased susceptibility to intracellular pathogens and viral infections.
- **Differentials:** Remember to differentiate TYK2 deficiency (affecting IFN-γ signaling) from other primary immunodeficiencies affecting T cell activation (e.g., CD3ζ defects, SCID), B cell function (e.g., XLA), or innate immunity (e.g., TLR defects). The specific genetic defect and cytokine receptor involvement are key to diagnosis.

<a id='question-074'></a>

### Question 074: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 24 (signaling)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Deficiencies in intracellular signaling pathways, specifically involving tyrosine kinases like Syk, lead to impaired B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling, resulting in defective B-cell development and function, manifesting as recurrent bacterial infections and hypogammaglobulinemia. (细胞内信号通路缺陷，特别是 Syk 激酶缺陷，导致 B 细胞受体 (BCR) 信号传导受损，进而影响 B 细胞的发育和功能，表现为复发性细菌感染和低球蛋白血症。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia, otitis media, and sinusitis, starting at 6 months of age after maternal antibody protection waned. He has not received any live vaccines due to concerns about potential complications. His medical history is otherwise unremarkable, with no history of severe viral infections, opportunistic infections, or autoimmune manifestations. Family history reveals that his maternal uncle also experienced recurrent infections in childhood. Physical examination shows normal growth and development, but reveals chronic rhinitis and slightly enlarged cervical lymph nodes. Laboratory investigations show markedly reduced levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM) and a severely decreased number of peripheral B cells (<1% of lymphocytes). T-cell numbers and function appear normal. Flow cytometry analysis of B cells reveals normal expression of surface markers like CD19, CD20, and CD21, but absent expression of CD79b (Igα) and impaired phosphorylation of downstream signaling molecules upon BCR stimulation. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *Syk* gene.

Which of the following intracellular signaling pathways is most directly impaired in this patient?

(A) JAK-STAT pathway involved in cytokine receptor signaling
(B) NF-κB pathway involved in inflammatory responses
(C) PI3K pathway involved in B-cell survival and proliferation
(D) Syk-dependent pathway involved in BCR signaling
(Syk 依赖的 B 细胞受体 (BCR) 信号通路)
(E) MyD88-dependent pathway involved in TLR signaling

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections starting after 6 months:** Suggests a defect in humoral immunity (antibody production) as maternal IgG wanes. This points towards a B-cell defect or antibody deficiency.
- **Markedly reduced levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM):** Confirms panhypogammaglobulinemia, indicating a severe defect in B-cell development or function affecting all antibody classes.
- **Severely decreased number of peripheral B cells (<1%):** Points towards a defect in B-cell development (lymphopoiesis) rather than just a functional defect in mature B cells.
- **Normal T-cell numbers and function:** Rules out combined immunodeficiencies (SCID) or T-cell specific defects.
- **Flow cytometry: Normal CD19, CD20, CD21 expression, absent CD79b (Igα), impaired phosphorylation upon BCR stimulation:** CD19, CD20, and CD21 are pan-B cell markers. CD79b (Igα) forms the BCR complex with IgD. Absence of CD79b and impaired phosphorylation indicate a defect in BCR signaling initiation or downstream events.
- **Genetic testing: Homozygous mutation in the *Syk* gene:** *Syk* encodes Spleen tyrosine kinase, a crucial non-receptor tyrosine kinase involved in downstream signaling of the BCR complex.
- **Family history:** Suggests an autosomal recessive inheritance pattern, common for many primary immunodeficiencies.

These clues collectively point to a primary immunodeficiency affecting B-cell development due to a defect in the BCR signaling pathway, specifically involving Syk.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **B-cell Receptor (BCR) Signaling:** The BCR complex consists of membrane-bound immunoglobulin (IgM/IgD) associated with signaling molecules CD79a (Igα) and CD79b (Igβ) and the Igα/Igβ heterodimer. Upon antigen binding, the BCR undergoes clustering and activation.
- **Syk Kinase Role:** The cytoplasmic tails of CD79b (Igβ) contain immunoreceptor tyrosine-based activation motifs (ITAMs). Antigen binding leads to the recruitment and activation of Src family kinases (like Lyn), which phosphorylate the ITAMs on CD79b. This phosphorylation creates docking sites for Syk.
- **Downstream Signaling:** Syk binds to the phosphorylated ITAMs and undergoes autophosphorylation, becoming catalytically active. Activated Syk then phosphorylates other downstream signaling molecules, including BLNK (B-cell linker), which recruits additional signaling adaptors and enzymes like PI3K and PLCγ2.
- **Consequences of Syk Deficiency:** Lack of functional Syk prevents the phosphorylation cascade initiated by BCR engagement. This leads to:
    - Impaired B-cell development: Pro-B and pre-B cells fail to mature into immature and then mature B cells.
    - Defective B-cell activation: Mature B cells cannot respond effectively to antigen stimulation.
    - Absence of antibody production: Leads to panhypogammaglobulinemia.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** The resulting humoral immunodeficiency manifests as recurrent bacterial infections, particularly encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*), affecting the respiratory tract (pneumonia, otitis media, sinusitis) and other sites.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** This clinical picture is characteristic of X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA) caused by mutations in *BTK* (Bruton's tyrosine kinase), another crucial kinase in BCR signaling, but XLA is X-linked and affects males. Syk deficiency is autosomal recessive. Both result in a block in B-cell development at the pre-B cell stage.

##### 3. Syk Pathway Specifics:
- Syk is a non-receptor tyrosine kinase belonging to the spleen tyrosine kinase family.
- It is essential for signaling downstream of various receptors, including the BCR, Fc receptors (FcγRIIB), and certain chemokine receptors.
- In the BCR pathway, Syk activation is critical for B-cell development, activation, proliferation, differentiation, and antibody production.
- Mutations in *Syk* lead to a severe combined immunodeficiency characterized by absent B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia, similar phenotypically to XLA but with autosomal recessive inheritance.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **JAK-STAT pathway:** This pathway is activated by cytokine receptors (e.g., interleukins, interferons, growth hormone). It is crucial for T-cell and NK-cell development and function, as well as hematopoiesis. Defects in JAK-STAT signaling (e.g., JAK3 deficiency) cause SCID, characterized by absent T cells and NK cells, but typically normal B-cell numbers (though B-cell function may be impaired due to lack of T-cell help). This patient has normal T cells and absent B cells, making JAK-STAT deficiency unlikely.
- (B) **NF-κB pathway:** This pathway is activated by various stimuli, including TLRs, cytokines (like TNFα), and the BCR. It plays a central role in inflammation, cell survival, and immune responses. While NF-κB is involved in B-cell activation, the primary defect described (absent CD79b, impaired BCR phosphorylation, Syk mutation) points specifically to an earlier step in the BCR signaling cascade initiated by Syk, rather than a general defect in NF-κB activation. Defects in NF-κB components (e.g., NEMO deficiency) typically cause ectodermal dysplasia and immunodeficiency, often with susceptibility to viral infections and autoimmunity, which are not prominent features here.
- (C) **PI3K pathway:** Phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase (PI3K) is a downstream effector of Syk in the BCR pathway. It is involved in B-cell survival, proliferation, and differentiation. While Syk deficiency ultimately affects PI3K activation, the *direct* impairment is at the level of Syk itself. Defects in PI3K isoforms (e.g., PI3Kδ syndrome) cause immunodeficiency, autoimmunity, and predisposition to lymphoma, but the specific findings of absent CD79b and the confirmed Syk mutation point directly to Syk as the primary defect.
- (E) **MyD88-dependent pathway:** MyD88 (Myeloid differentiation primary response 88) is an adaptor protein essential for signaling downstream of most Toll-like receptors (TLRs) and the IL-1 receptor. It is crucial for innate immunity and the activation of NF-κB and MAP kinases. Defects in MyD88 cause susceptibility primarily to pyogenic bacterial infections (especially *Streptococcus pneumoniae* and *Staphylococcus aureus*) due to impaired innate immune responses, but typically do not affect B-cell development or immunoglobulin levels significantly. This patient has a profound B-cell defect and panhypogammaglobulinemia, making MyD88 deficiency unlikely.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Syk Deficiency (Option D) | JAK-STAT Deficiency (Option A) | NF-κB Deficiency (Option B) | PI3K Deficiency (Option C) | MyD88 Deficiency (Option E) |
|-------------------------|--------------------------|-----------------------------|---------------------------|--------------------------|---------------------------|
| **Primary Defect**      | Syk Kinase               | JAK Kinase (e.g., JAK3)      | NF-κB Components (e.g., NEMO) | PI3K isoforms (e.g., PI3Kδ) | MyD88 Adaptor Protein     |
| **Affected Cells**      | B cells                  | T cells, NK cells            | Multiple cell types       | B cells, T cells         | Innate immune cells       |
| **B-cell Numbers**      | Absent/Severely Reduced  | Normal                      | Normal/Reduced            | Normal/Reduced           | Normal                    |
| **Immunoglobulins**     | Panhypogammaglobulinemia | Normal/Reduced (due to lack of T help) | Normal/Reduced            | Normal/Reduced           | Normal                    |
| **Key Pathway**         | BCR Signaling            | Cytokine Receptor Signaling | Inflammatory Responses   | B-cell Survival/Prolif. | TLR/IL-1R Signaling       |
| **Inheritance**         | Autosomal Recessive      | Autosomal Recessive/X-linked | Autosomal Dominant/Recessive | Autosomal Dominant       | Autosomal Recessive       |
| **Clinical Features**   | Recurrent bacterial infections | Severe infections (viral, bacterial, opportunistic) | Ectodermal dysplasia, infections | Immunodeficiency, autoimmunity, lymphoma | Pyogenic bacterial infections |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Syk kinase is essential for downstream signaling of the B-cell receptor (BCR). Mutations in *Syk* cause autosomal recessive B-cell immunodeficiency characterized by absent B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia, mimicking XLA (caused by *BTK* mutations) but with a different inheritance pattern. Key differentials include XLA (BTK defect, X-linked), other signaling defects (e.g., JAK3, PI3K), and defects in B-cell development factors. Remember that Syk deficiency specifically impairs the BCR signaling cascade initiated by antigen binding.
