<a id='question-075'></a>

### Question 075: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 25 (pathway)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Defective intracellular trafficking of lysosomal enzymes due to mutations in the *CTPSF4* gene leads to impaired phagolysosome formation, resulting in susceptibility to intracellular pathogens, particularly *Salmonella* species. (CTPSF4基因突变导致溶酶体酶胞内运输缺陷，溶酶体-吞噬体融合受损，易感于胞内病原体，特别是沙门氏菌。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent episodes of severe, bloody diarrhea, abdominal pain, and fever, beginning shortly after birth. His growth has been significantly impaired, and he exhibits developmental delay. He has been hospitalized multiple times for suspected bacterial gastroenteritis, with stool cultures consistently positive for *Salmonella enterica* serovar Typhimurium. Initial workup revealed normal immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM), normal complement levels (C3, C4), and normal numbers of T cells, B cells, and NK cells. However, flow cytometry showed impaired expression of lysosomal-associated membrane protein 1 (LAMP-1) on neutrophils. A bone marrow biopsy showed normal cellularity but revealed giant granules within neutrophils and monocytes. Genetic testing confirmed a homozygous mutation in the *CTPSF4* gene.

Which of the following intracellular pathways is most likely defective in this patient, explaining his susceptibility to *Salmonella* infection?

(A) The mannose-6-phosphate (M6P) pathway, leading to impaired trafficking of lysosomal hydrolases.
(B) The autophagy pathway, leading to impaired degradation of cytoplasmic proteins and organelles.
(C) The endosomal sorting complexes required for transport (ESCRT) pathway, leading to impaired multivesicular body formation.
(D) The phagolysosome fusion pathway, leading to impaired killing of phagocytosed bacteria.
(E) The antigen processing and presentation pathway, leading to impaired T cell activation.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe *Salmonella* infections:** Points towards a defect in intracellular killing, particularly of phagocytosed bacteria. *Salmonella* is a facultative intracellular pathogen that survives and replicates within macrophages.
- **Early onset, bloody diarrhea, abdominal pain, fever:** Classic symptoms of severe salmonellosis, often seen in immunocompromised individuals.
- **Impaired growth and developmental delay:** Suggests a chronic, debilitating condition affecting nutrient absorption and overall health.
- **Normal immunoglobulin levels, complement levels, and lymphocyte counts:** Rules out common primary immunodeficiencies affecting humoral immunity (e.g., XLA, CVID), complement (e.g., C3 deficiency), or lymphocyte development/function (e.g., SCID).
- **Impaired LAMP-1 expression on neutrophils:** LAMP-1 is a marker for lysosomes. Impaired expression suggests a defect in lysosomal function or trafficking.
- **Giant granules in neutrophils and monocytes:** This is a characteristic finding in certain lysosomal storage disorders and primary immunodeficiencies affecting lysosomal function, indicating abnormal granule formation or fusion.
- **Homozygous mutation in the *CTPSF4* gene:** This gene encodes the Chediak-Philani syndrome protein 4, which is involved in lysosomal trafficking and fusion. Mutations cause Chediak-Philani syndrome (CHS).
- **Connecting the dots:** The clinical picture (recurrent *Salmonella*), laboratory findings (normal Ig, complement, cell counts; abnormal LAMP-1, giant granules), and genetic defect (*CTPSF4* mutation) all point towards Chediak-Philani syndrome. CHS is characterized by defective phagolysosome formation, impairing the ability of phagocytes to kill ingested microbes.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chediak-Philani Syndrome (CHS):** An autosomal recessive disorder caused by mutations in the *LYST* (or *CHS1*) gene, which encodes a protein involved in regulating lysosomal trafficking and fusion. The *CTPSF4* gene product is also involved in this pathway, and mutations can cause a similar phenotype.
- **Pathophysiology:** The defect leads to impaired fusion of lysosomes with phagosomes (phagolysosome formation) and other intracellular vesicles. This results in the formation of giant lysosomes and granules in various cells (neutrophils, monocytes, melanocytes, platelets, neurons).
- **Immune Consequences:** Phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages) cannot effectively kill ingested bacteria because the phagosome fails to fuse with lysosomes, which contain degradative enzymes. This leads to increased susceptibility to infections, particularly by catalase-positive organisms like *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Serratia marcescens*, as well as *Salmonella*.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Recurrent pyogenic infections (skin, respiratory tract, GI tract), partial oculocutaneous albinism (due to impaired melanosome trafficking), bleeding diathesis (due to platelet dysfunction), and progressive neurological dysfunction.
- **Phagolysosome Fusion:** This process is crucial for intracellular killing. After a phagocyte engulfs a pathogen, the phagosome matures and fuses with lysosomes, forming a phagolysosome. Lysosomal enzymes (proteases, lipases, nucleases) and reactive oxygen species (ROS) within the phagolysosome degrade the pathogen. Defective fusion prevents this killing mechanism.
- **Mannose-6-Phosphate (M6P) Pathway:** Involved in targeting lysosomal hydrolases from the Golgi apparatus to lysosomes. Defects cause mucolipidosis types II and III, characterized by lysosomal storage but not typically the phagolysosome fusion defect seen in CHS.
- **Autophagy Pathway:** Involved in degrading cytoplasmic components and pathogens (xenophagy). While related to lysosomal function, the primary defect in CHS is phagolysosome fusion, not autophagy itself.
- **ESCRT Pathway:** Involved in forming intraluminal vesicles within multivesicular bodies (MVBs), which then fuse with lysosomes. Defects are linked to neurological disorders and cancer, not typically CHS.
- **Antigen Processing and Presentation:** Involves processing antigens within lysosomes/phagolysosomes and presenting them on MHC molecules. While lysosomal function is involved, the primary defect in CHS is the fusion step, not the antigen processing machinery itself, although impaired killing can indirectly affect antigen presentation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): The mannose-6-phosphate (M6P) pathway is essential for targeting newly synthesized lysosomal hydrolases from the Golgi to the endosomes/lysosomes. Defects in this pathway (e.g., mucolipidosis II/III) lead to accumulation of undegraded substrates in lysosomes due to lack of these enzymes, but do not primarily affect phagolysosome fusion or cause the characteristic giant granules seen in CHS. The vignette describes impaired *fusion*, not targeting of enzymes.
- (B): The autophagy pathway is responsible for degrading cytoplasmic components and pathogens within autophagosomes, which then fuse with lysosomes (autolysosomes). While autophagy is a lysosomal degradation pathway, the primary defect in CHS is the fusion of *phagosomes* with lysosomes, not the autophagy pathway itself. Defects in autophagy are associated with different diseases (e.g., neurodegenerative disorders).
- (C): The endosomal sorting complexes required for transport (ESCRT) pathway is involved in the formation of multivesicular bodies (MVBs) by budding intraluminal vesicles. MVBs then fuse with lysosomes. Defects in ESCRT components are associated with neurological disorders and cancer, not the specific phagolysosome fusion defect and giant granules characteristic of CHS.
- (E): The antigen processing and presentation pathway involves the degradation of antigens within lysosomes/phagolysosomes and loading of peptides onto MHC molecules. While this pathway relies on lysosomal function, the primary defect in CHS is the *fusion* of the phagosome and lysosome, preventing the delivery of degradative enzymes to the phagocytosed microbe. The vignette focuses on the killing defect, not the antigen presentation process itself, although impaired killing can secondarily affect antigen presentation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chediak-Philani Syndrome (CHS) | Mucolipidosis II/III (M6P defect) | Autophagy Defects | ESCRT Defects |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :---------------- | :------------ |
| **Primary Defect**      | Phagolysosome fusion           | Lysosomal enzyme targeting        | Autophagosome-lysosome fusion | MVB formation |
| **Gene Involved**       | *LYST* (or *CTPSF4*)           | *SLC35C1*                         | Various           | Various       |
| **Key Finding**         | Giant granules/lysosomes       | Lysosomal storage, coarse facies | N/A               | N/A           |
| **Immune Manifestation** | Recurrent bacterial infections | Lysosomal storage, not primary immune defect | N/A               | N/A           |
| **Pathogen Susceptibility** | *Staph*, *Salmonella*, *Serratia* | N/A                               | N/A               | N/A           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Chediak-Philani syndrome is a primary immunodeficiency characterized by defective phagolysosome fusion due to mutations affecting lysosomal trafficking (e.g., *LYST* or *CTPSF4* genes). This leads to impaired intracellular killing, giant granules in leukocytes, partial albinism, and recurrent infections, especially with catalase-positive organisms. Differentiating CHS from other lysosomal storage disorders or defects in other intracellular pathways requires understanding the specific function affected (phagolysosome fusion vs. enzyme targeting vs. autophagy vs. MVB formation).

<a id='question-076'></a>

### Question 076: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 26 (leading)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Defective phagocyte NADPH oxidase complex leads to impaired oxidative burst and recurrent bacterial/fungal infections, particularly catalase-positive organisms, forming granulomas. (NADPH 氧化酶复合体缺陷导致氧化爆发受损，并出现复发性细菌/真菌感染，特别是过氧化物酶阳性生物，形成肉芽肿。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents with a history of recurrent, severe bacterial and fungal infections since infancy, including pneumonia, skin abscesses, lymphadenitis, and osteomyelitis. His infections are often caused by *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, and *Aspergillus* species. Physical examination reveals multiple draining skin lesions and enlarged cervical lymph nodes. A biopsy of a lymph node shows granulomas with giant cells, but no caseous necrosis. Laboratory tests show normal serum immunoglobulin levels and lymphocyte counts. A neutrophil function test, specifically the dihydrorhodamine (DHR) assay, shows markedly reduced fluorescence upon stimulation with phorbol myristate acetate (PMA).

Which of the following molecular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells
(B) Defective gamma chain (γc) subunit of cytokine receptors
(C) Defective NADPH oxidase complex subunits
(D) Defective adenosine deaminase (ADA) enzyme activity
(E) Defective complement component C3

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe bacterial and fungal infections since infancy:** Suggests a primary immunodeficiency affecting innate immunity, particularly phagocytes.
- **Specific pathogens (*S. aureus*, *S. marcescens*, *B. cepacia*, *Aspergillus*):** These organisms are catalase-positive. Catalase breaks down hydrogen peroxide (H₂O₂), which is a key product of the phagocytic oxidative burst. Phagocytes need to generate reactive oxygen species (ROS) like superoxide (O₂⁻) and H₂O₂ to kill catalase-positive organisms.
- **Granulomas with giant cells:** Indicates an attempt by the immune system to wall off organisms that cannot be effectively killed by phagocytes, characteristic of Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD).
- **Normal serum immunoglobulin levels and lymphocyte counts:** Rules out humoral immunodeficiencies (like XLA, CVID) and severe combined immunodeficiencies (SCID) affecting lymphocyte development.
- **Dihydrorhodamine (DHR) assay shows reduced fluorescence:** This test measures the production of superoxide radicals (O₂⁻) by neutrophils. Reduced fluorescence indicates a defect in the respiratory burst, specifically the NADPH oxidase complex.
- **Putting it together:** The clinical picture of recurrent catalase-positive infections, granuloma formation, and a defective respiratory burst (confirmed by DHR assay) strongly points to Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). CGD is caused by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD):** An inherited primary immunodeficiency characterized by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex.
- **NADPH Oxidase Complex:** A multi-subunit enzyme complex located in the phagosome membrane of phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages). Its function is to generate superoxide radicals (O₂⁻) by transferring electrons from NADPH to molecular oxygen (O₂).
- **Respiratory Burst:** The process by which phagocytes rapidly produce ROS (superoxide, hydrogen peroxide, hydroxyl radicals) to kill ingested microbes. The NADPH oxidase complex is essential for initiating this process.
- **Mechanism in CGD:** Mutations in genes encoding subunits of the NADPH oxidase complex (e.g., *CYBB* for gp91phox, *CYBA* for p22phox, *NCF1* for p47phox, *NCF2* for p67phox, *NCF4* for p40phox) lead to a non-functional or deficient complex. This impairs the production of superoxide and other ROS.
- **Consequences:** Phagocytes can ingest microbes but cannot effectively kill catalase-positive bacteria and fungi, leading to recurrent infections and granuloma formation as the body attempts to contain the infection.
- **DHR Assay:** A flow cytometry-based test used to diagnose CGD. DHR is a non-fluorescent dye that is converted to a fluorescent product (rhodamine 123) by superoxide radicals. In CGD patients, neutrophils fail to produce superoxide upon stimulation, resulting in little or no fluorescence.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells:** This causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal B cell numbers but cannot undergo class switching or affinity maturation, leading to high IgM levels and low IgG, IgA, IgE. They are susceptible to opportunistic infections, particularly *Pneumocystis jirovecii*. This does not fit the clinical picture of recurrent bacterial/fungal infections with granulomas and a defective respiratory burst.
- (B) **Defective gamma chain (γc) subunit of cytokine receptors:** This causes X-linked Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (X-SCID). The γc chain is shared by receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21. Deficiency leads to impaired T cell and NK cell development, resulting in severe susceptibility to a wide range of bacterial, viral, and fungal infections early in life. Lymphocyte counts are typically very low. This does not fit the normal lymphocyte counts and specific infection pattern seen in the vignette.
- (D) **Defective adenosine deaminase (ADA) enzyme activity:** This causes ADA-SCID, an autosomal recessive form of SCID. ADA deficiency leads to the accumulation of toxic metabolites (deoxyadenosine) that are particularly harmful to lymphocytes, resulting in severe lymphopenia and impaired T, B, and NK cell function. Similar to X-SCID, this presents with severe, broad-spectrum infections early in life and low lymphocyte counts, unlike the patient described.
- (E) **Defective complement component C3:** C3 deficiency (or deficiency of other early complement components like C1, C2, C4) leads to impaired opsonization and lysis of encapsulated bacteria. Patients are susceptible to recurrent infections with *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*, and *Neisseria meningitidis*. It does not typically cause granulomas or susceptibility to catalase-positive organisms like *Aspergillus* or *Serratia*. The respiratory burst is normal in complement deficiencies.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | X-linked Hyper-IgM Syndrome | X-linked SCID | ADA-SCID | C3 Deficiency |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :----------- | :------- | :----------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | NADPH Oxidase Complex              | CD40L                       | γc subunit   | ADA      | C3           |
| **Immune Cell Affected** | Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages) | T cells, B cells            | T cells, NK cells | Lymphocytes | N/A (Complement) |
| **Key Pathogens**       | Catalase-positive (Staph, Serratia, Burkholderia, Aspergillus) | Opportunistic (Pneumocystis) | Broad (Bacteria, Virus, Fungi) | Broad (Bacteria, Virus, Fungi) | Encapsulated bacteria (Strep pneumo, H. flu, Neisseria) |
| **Hallmark Feature**    | Granulomas, Defective Respiratory Burst | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE   | Severe Lymphopenia | Lymphopenia | Impaired opsonization/lysis |
| **DHR Assay**           | Reduced Fluorescence                | Normal                      | Normal       | Normal   | Normal       |
| **Inheritance**         | X-linked (most common), AR          | X-linked                   | X-linked     | AR      | AR/AD        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CGD is a primary immunodeficiency affecting phagocyte function due to defects in the NADPH oxidase complex, leading to impaired oxidative burst and susceptibility to catalase-positive organisms. The DHR assay is diagnostic. Key differentials include other primary immunodeficiencies affecting phagocytes (e.g., Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency) or lymphocytes (SCID, CVID, Hyper-IgM), which present with different infection patterns and immunological findings. Remember the specific pathogens and the hallmark granuloma formation in CGD.

<a id='question-077'></a>

### Question 077: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 27 (impaired)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular basis and clinical manifestations of defects in the **interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) signaling pathway**, specifically focusing on the role of **STAT1** in mediating IFN-γ responses, leading to susceptibility to intracellular pathogens, particularly mycobacteria and certain viruses. (考察干扰素γ信号通路缺陷，特别是STAT1在介导干扰素γ反应中的作用，导致对胞内病原体，特别是分枝杆菌和某些病毒的易感性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy from rural Mississippi presents with a 6-month history of recurrent, severe respiratory infections, including pneumonia and sinusitis. His infections are often caused by atypical organisms, including *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* and *Staphylococcus aureus*. He has also experienced chronic diarrhea and failure to thrive. Physical examination reveals multiple non-caseating granulomas in the lungs and lymph nodes, as well as persistent oral candidiasis. Laboratory studies show normal immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE) and normal T-cell counts (CD3+, CD4+, CD8+). However, his T-cells exhibit markedly reduced phosphorylation of STAT1 upon stimulation with IFN-γ *in vitro*. Genetic testing reveals compound heterozygous mutations in the *STAT1* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying defect in this patient's immune system?

(A) Impaired production of interleukin-12 (IL-12) by antigen-presenting cells.
(B) Defective formation of the phagosome-lysosome fusion in neutrophils.
(C) Inability of macrophages to produce reactive oxygen species (ROS).
(D) Defective intracellular trafficking of the mannose-6-phosphate receptor (M6PR).
(E) Impaired activation of the complement cascade via the alternative pathway.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Recurrent severe respiratory infections (pneumonia, sinusitis) with atypical organisms (*Mycobacterium tuberculosis*, *Staphylococcus aureus*)**: Suggests a defect in cell-mediated immunity, particularly against intracellular pathogens. *M. tuberculosis* requires Th1 responses (IFN-γ) for macrophage activation. *S. aureus* can cause severe infections in patients with impaired phagocyte function or T-cell defects.
*   **Chronic diarrhea and failure to thrive**: Common features of severe primary immunodeficiencies, often related to chronic infections or malabsorption.
*   **Non-caseating granulomas**: While granulomas are typically associated with Th1 responses (like in tuberculosis), *non-caseating* granulomas can be seen in certain immunodeficiencies, including defects in IFN-γ signaling, as the granulomas form but lack the central caseous necrosis due to impaired macrophage activation.
*   **Persistent oral candidiasis**: Suggests a defect in T-cell immunity, as T-cells are crucial for controlling fungal infections like *Candida*.
*   **Normal immunoglobulin levels and T-cell counts**: Rules out common variable immunodeficiency (CVID), X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), and severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) which typically present with low immunoglobulins or low T-cell counts, respectively.
*   **Reduced STAT1 phosphorylation upon IFN-γ stimulation**: This is the key laboratory finding. IFN-γ signals through the JAK-STAT pathway. IFN-γ binds to its receptor, leading to JAK1/JAK2 activation, which phosphorylates STAT1. Phosphorylated STAT1 dimerizes, translocates to the nucleus, and activates transcription of genes involved in macrophage activation, Th1 differentiation, and antiviral responses. Reduced phosphorylation indicates a defect downstream of the IFN-γ receptor, specifically in the JAK-STAT pathway.
*   **Compound heterozygous mutations in the *STAT1* gene**: Confirms the genetic basis for the impaired IFN-γ signaling. STAT1 mutations lead to autosomal dominant hyper-IgE syndrome (HIES), also known as Job's syndrome, characterized by recurrent staphylococcal abscesses, eczema, elevated IgE, and skeletal abnormalities, but also susceptibility to mycobacterial infections due to impaired IFN-γ signaling. The presentation here is more severe, potentially reflecting compound heterozygosity or other genetic modifiers.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **IFN-γ Signaling Pathway**: IFN-γ is a crucial cytokine for cell-mediated immunity. It activates macrophages to kill intracellular pathogens (like *M. tuberculosis*) via production of ROS and nitric oxide. It promotes Th1 differentiation and inhibits Th2 differentiation. It enhances MHC class I and II expression. The signaling pathway involves binding to the IFN-γ receptor (IFNGR), which recruits JAK1 and JAK2 kinases. These kinases phosphorylate tyrosine residues on the receptor, creating docking sites for STAT1. JAKs then phosphorylate STAT1 on tyrosine 701. Phosphorylated STAT1 (p-STAT1) dimerizes, translocates to the nucleus, and binds to IFN-γ-activated sequence (GAS) elements in the promoter regions of target genes (e.g., *IRF1*, *HLA-DR*, *TAP1*, *TAP2*, *NOS2*, *iNOS*).
*   **STAT1 Deficiency**: Mutations in *STAT1* impair this signaling cascade. Lack of p-STAT1 prevents the transcription of IFN-γ-responsive genes. This leads to defective macrophage activation, impaired Th1 responses, and increased susceptibility to intracellular pathogens, particularly mycobacteria and certain viruses (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Candida albicans*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Haemophilus influenzae*, *Aspergillus fumigatus*, *Pneumocystis jirovecii*). The clinical picture aligns with impaired IFN-γ signaling.
*   **IL-12 Production**: IL-12 is produced by antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like macrophages and dendritic cells in response to intracellular pathogens. IL-12 is essential for driving Th1 differentiation and IFN-γ production by T-cells and NK cells. Defects in IL-12 production (e.g., due to mutations in *IL12B*, *IL12RB1*, *STAT4*) or IL-12 signaling (e.g., *IL12RB1*, *STAT4*) also lead to susceptibility to intracellular pathogens, particularly mycobacteria, but the key laboratory finding here is specifically impaired *response* to IFN-γ (reduced STAT1 phosphorylation), not impaired IL-12 production itself. While IL-12 is upstream of IFN-γ, the direct defect identified is in the IFN-γ signaling pathway.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Impaired production of interleukin-12 (IL-12) by antigen-presenting cells**: Defects in IL-12 production or signaling (e.g., mutations in *IL12B*, *IL12RB1*, *STAT4*) cause Mendelian Susceptibility to Mycobacterial Disease (MSMD). Patients with MSMD have impaired Th1 responses and are susceptible to mycobacteria and *Salmonella*. However, the vignette specifically points to a defect *downstream* of IFN-γ, as evidenced by the reduced STAT1 phosphorylation upon IFN-γ stimulation. IL-12 is upstream of IFN-γ signaling. Therefore, while clinically similar in some aspects (mycobacterial susceptibility), the specific laboratory finding points away from this option.
- (B) **Defective formation of the phagosome-lysosome fusion in neutrophils**: This defect is characteristic of Chediak-Higashi syndrome, caused by mutations in the *LYST* gene. Patients have recurrent pyogenic infections (often *S. aureus*), partial oculocutaneous albinism, and giant granules in neutrophils and other cells. While *S. aureus* infections are present, the specific defect in IFN-γ signaling and the presence of non-caseating granulomas are not typical features of Chediak-Higashi syndrome.
- (C) **Inability of macrophages to produce reactive oxygen species (ROS)**: This defect is seen in Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD), caused by mutations in genes encoding subunits of the NADPH oxidase complex (e.g., *CYBB*, *CYBA*, *NCF1*, *NCF2*, *NCF4*). Patients have recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms (e.g., *S. aureus*, *Aspergillus*, *Serratia*, *Burkholderia*, *Nocardia*) because macrophages cannot generate ROS to kill phagocytosed microbes. While *S. aureus* infections are present, CGD patients typically have *caseating* granulomas, and the defect is in ROS production, not IFN-γ signaling. The reduced STAT1 phosphorylation finding is inconsistent with CGD.
- (D) **Defective intracellular trafficking of the mannose-6-phosphate receptor (M6PR)**: This defect is characteristic of Mucolipidosis II (I-cell disease) or Mucolipidosis III (pseudo-Hurler polydystrophy), lysosomal storage disorders. These are not primary immunodeficiencies and do not typically present with susceptibility to mycobacteria or impaired IFN-γ signaling.
- (E) **Impaired activation of the complement cascade via the alternative pathway**: Defects in the alternative pathway (e.g., Factor B, Factor D, Properdin deficiency) lead to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria, particularly *Neisseria* species. This does not explain the susceptibility to intracellular pathogens like *M. tuberculosis* or the impaired IFN-γ signaling observed in the patient.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | STAT1 Deficiency (Hyper-IgE Syndrome) | IL-12/STAT4 Deficiency (MSMD) | CGD (NADPH Oxidase Defect) | Chediak-Higashi (LYST Defect) |
| :-------------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :----------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**          | IFN-γ Signaling (JAK-STAT pathway)     | IL-12 Production/Signaling    | NADPH Oxidase               | Phagosome-Lysosome Fusion     |
| **Key Lab Finding**         | ↓ STAT1 Phosphorylation (post-IFNγ)    | ↓ IL-12 Production            | ↓ DHR/Nitroblue Tetrazolium Test | Giant Granules                 |
| **Typical Infections**      | *S. aureus*, Mycobacteria, Candida, Viruses | Mycobacteria, Salmonella     | Catalase+ Bacteria, Fungi   | *S. aureus*, Pyogenic Bacteria |
| **Granulomas**              | Non-caseating                          | Caseating                     | Caseating                  | Absent                         |
| **Other Features**          | Eczema, High IgE, Skeletal Abnormalities | None specific                 | None specific               | Albinism, Neuropathy           |
| **Genetic Basis**           | *STAT1* mutations                      | *IL12B*, *IL12RB1*, *STAT4*    | *CYBB*, *CYBA*, *NCF* genes | *LYST* gene                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The vignette describes a patient with recurrent infections by intracellular pathogens (*M. tuberculosis*, *S. aureus*), non-caseating granulomas, and impaired IFN-γ signaling (reduced STAT1 phosphorylation). This constellation points specifically to a defect in the IFN-γ signaling pathway, most commonly caused by mutations in *STAT1*. Key differentials include defects in IL-12 production/signaling (MSMD) and phagocyte defects like CGD and Chediak-Higashi syndrome, which have distinct clinical features and laboratory findings. Remember that IFN-γ is crucial for macrophage activation and Th1 responses.

<a id='question-078'></a>

### Question 078: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 28 (function:)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the functional defect in Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS), a primary immunodeficiency characterized by eczema, thrombocytopenia, and recurrent infections, specifically focusing on the role of the WAS protein (WASP) in actin cytoskeleton rearrangement and immune cell function. (本题考察Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS）的功能缺陷，这是一种原发性免疫缺陷病，其特征是湿疹、血小板减少症和复发性感染，重点考察WASP蛋白在肌动蛋白细胞骨架重排和免疫细胞功能中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with a history of severe, persistent eczema since infancy, frequent bleeding episodes (petechiae, purpura) requiring platelet transfusions, and recurrent sinopulmonary infections (otitis media, pneumonia). His mother reports that he has had difficulty gaining weight and often appears fatigued. Family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died at age 10 from lymphoma. Physical examination reveals widespread eczematous lesions, pallor, and mild splenomegaly. Laboratory studies show thrombocytopenia (platelet count 20,000/µL) with small platelet size, low serum IgM levels, and normal or elevated IgA and IgG levels. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals decreased expression of CD43 (sialophorin) on T cells and platelets. A bone marrow biopsy shows normal cellularity but decreased megakaryocytes. Which of the following molecular pathways is most directly impaired in this patient's condition?

(A) T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling complex assembly
(B) B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling complex assembly
(C) Actin cytoskeleton rearrangement required for immune synapse formation and platelet function
(D) Complement activation pathway leading to opsonization
(E) Neutrophil chemotaxis mediated by integrin activation

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy.
- **Key Symptoms:** Severe eczema, recurrent sinopulmonary infections, bleeding episodes (petechiae, purpura), thrombocytopenia, small platelets, low IgM, normal/high IgA/IgG, fatigue, failure to thrive.
- **Family History:** Maternal uncle died young from lymphoma (suggests X-linked inheritance).
- **Physical Exam:** Eczema, pallor, splenomegaly.
- **Lab Findings:** Thrombocytopenia (low platelet count), small platelets, low IgM, normal/high IgA/IgG, decreased CD43 expression on T cells and platelets.
- **Bone Marrow:** Normal cellularity, decreased megakaryocytes (consistent with platelet production defect).
- **Diagnosis:** The constellation of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), recurrent infections, low IgM, and X-linked inheritance pattern strongly points towards Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most directly impaired molecular pathway*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS):** An X-linked recessive primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene, which encodes the Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome protein (WASP).
- **WASP Function:** WASP is a crucial cytoplasmic protein involved in actin cytoskeleton organization and signaling pathways in hematopoietic cells, particularly lymphocytes and platelets. It interacts with various signaling molecules and actin-binding proteins (like Arp2/3 complex, WAVE/Scar complex) to regulate actin polymerization, cell motility, adhesion, and immune synapse formation.
- **Pathophysiology:**
    - **T cells:** WASP is essential for T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling, T-cell activation, proliferation, migration, and synapse formation. Defective WASP leads to impaired T-cell function, particularly affecting cell-mediated immunity.
    - **B cells:** WASP is involved in B-cell development, activation, and antibody production. Defective WASP contributes to impaired antibody responses, especially IgM production, and defective isotype switching.
    - **Platelets:** WASP is critical for platelet formation (thrombopoiesis) and function (adhesion, aggregation, motility). Defective WASP leads to thrombocytopenia and small platelets (microthrombocytopenia) due to impaired platelet pseudopod formation and cytoskeletal organization.
    - **Eczema:** The exact mechanism linking WASP deficiency to eczema is not fully understood but likely involves dysregulation of immune responses in the skin.
- **Molecular Pathway Impairment:** The core defect in WAS is the inability of WASP to properly regulate the actin cytoskeleton. This affects multiple cellular processes, including:
    - **Immune Synapse Formation:** Actin rearrangement is crucial for the formation of the immunological synapse between T cells and antigen-presenting cells (APCs), allowing for efficient signal transduction and T-cell activation.
    - **Cell Motility & Migration:** Actin dynamics are essential for cell movement, including lymphocyte migration to sites of infection and platelet function.
    - **Platelet Function:** Actin polymerization is required for platelet shape change, adhesion, and aggregation.
- **Therefore, the most direct molecular pathway impaired is actin cytoskeleton rearrangement.**

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling complex assembly:** While WASP deficiency *impairs* TCR signaling, it does so *indirectly* by disrupting the actin cytoskeleton rearrangement necessary for proper signal transduction and immune synapse formation. The primary defect isn't in the assembly of the TCR complex itself (which involves CD3, zeta chain, etc.), but rather in the downstream events mediated by actin.
- (B) **B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling complex assembly:** Similar to TCR signaling, BCR signaling is also affected in WAS, contributing to impaired antibody responses. However, the fundamental defect lies in the actin cytoskeleton, which is required for downstream signaling events and B-cell activation, not the initial assembly of the BCR complex itself.
- (D) **Complement activation pathway leading to opsonization:** WAS is primarily a defect in cellular immunity and platelet function, not the complement system. While recurrent infections might involve complement, the underlying genetic defect in WAS does not directly affect complement activation pathways.
- (E) **Neutrophil chemotaxis mediated by integrin activation:** Neutrophil chemotaxis relies on actin polymerization for cell movement and integrin activation for adhesion. While actin is involved, WASP deficiency primarily affects lymphocytes and platelets. Neutrophil function is generally less severely affected in WAS compared to T cells and platelets, and the primary defect is not specifically in neutrophil chemotaxis or integrin activation, but rather the broader actin cytoskeleton regulation in lymphocytes and platelets.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS) | Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) | DiGeorge Syndrome |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|-------------------|
| **Inheritance**      | X-linked Recessive              | Autosomal Recessive (most common)         | X-linked Recessive or Autosomal Recessive | X-linked Recessive               | Autosomal Dominant/De novo |
| **Key Features**     | Eczema, Thrombocytopenia (small platelets), Recurrent Infections, Low IgM | Severe infections early in life, Absent T & B cells | Recurrent bacterial/fungal infections, Granulomas | Recurrent bacterial infections, Absent B cells/Immunoglobulins | Hypocalcemia, Cardiac defects, Thymic hypoplasia, Facial dysmorphism |
| **Primary Defect**   | WASP protein, Actin cytoskeleton | Lymphocyte development (e.g., RAG1/2, IL2RG) | NADPH oxidase complex                 | BTK gene, B-cell development        | 22q11.2 deletion, Thymic development |
| **Affected Cells**   | T cells, B cells, Platelets      | T cells, B cells                          | Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages) | B cells                           | T cells (Thymus dependent) |
| **Actin Cytoskeleton**| **Directly Impaired**           | Generally Normal                          | Generally Normal                      | Generally Normal                  | Generally Normal  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is characterized by the triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections due to mutations in the *WAS* gene, leading to defective WASP protein function. WASP is crucial for actin cytoskeleton rearrangement in hematopoietic cells. The core defect is impaired actin dynamics, affecting immune synapse formation, cell motility, and platelet function. Differentiate WAS from other primary immunodeficiencies like XLA (absent B cells, normal platelets), SCID (severe T and B cell defects), CGD (defective phagocyte killing), and DiGeorge (T cell deficiency due to thymic hypoplasia).

<a id='question-079'></a>

### Question 079: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 29 (decreased)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies involving defects in intracellular signaling pathways, specifically focusing on the role of tyrosine kinases in lymphocyte development and function, and how mutations in these genes lead to susceptibility to specific infections. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是细胞内信号通路缺陷，具体关注酪氨酸激酶在淋巴细胞发育和功能中的作用，以及这些基因突变如何导致对特定感染的易感性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia, otitis media, and sinusitis, starting in infancy. He has a history of failure to thrive and chronic diarrhea. Physical examination reveals lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly. Laboratory studies show markedly decreased levels of serum immunoglobulins (IgG, IgA, IgM) and a significantly reduced number of circulating B lymphocytes. T lymphocyte counts and function appear normal. A bone marrow biopsy shows normal cellularity but reveals a paucity of B-cell precursors. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *BTK* gene.

Which of the following intracellular signaling pathways is most directly impaired in this patient?

(A) JAK-STAT pathway
(B) NF-κB pathway
(C) B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling pathway
(D) Toll-like receptor (TLR) signaling pathway
(E) Complement activation pathway

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections, failure to thrive, chronic diarrhea:** These symptoms in a young child point towards a significant immunodeficiency, particularly affecting humoral immunity, as encapsulated bacteria are common causes of these infections.
- **Lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly:** Suggests chronic inflammation or infection, potentially related to the immune defect.
- **Decreased serum immunoglobulins (IgG, IgA, IgM):** Indicates a defect in antibody production (hypogammaglobulinemia).
- **Reduced number of circulating B lymphocytes:** Points to a defect in B-cell development or survival.
- **Normal T lymphocyte counts and function:** Suggests the defect is primarily affecting the B-cell lineage.
- **Paucity of B-cell precursors in bone marrow:** Confirms a developmental defect in B cells.
- **Homozygous mutation in the *BTK* gene:** This is the key genetic clue. *BTK* encodes Bruton's tyrosine kinase, a crucial enzyme in B-cell development and signaling.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Bruton's Tyrosine Kinase (BTK):** BTK is a non-receptor tyrosine kinase essential for B-cell development beyond the pre-B cell stage in the bone marrow. It plays a critical role in transducing signals downstream of the B-cell receptor (BCR) and other receptors involved in B-cell maturation and survival (e.g., pre-BCR, IL-7R).
- **BCR Signaling Pathway:** Upon antigen binding, the BCR (composed of Igα/Igβ heterodimers and surface immunoglobulin) undergoes clustering and activation. This triggers the phosphorylation of immunoreceptor tyrosine-based activation motifs (ITAMs) on the Igα/Igβ chains. BTK is recruited to the phosphorylated ITAMs and phosphorylates downstream targets, including Syk (spleen tyrosine kinase). Syk activation initiates a signaling cascade involving PLCγ2, leading to calcium mobilization, NF-κB activation, and ultimately B-cell proliferation, differentiation, and antibody production.
- **X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA):** Mutations in *BTK* cause XLA, characterized by a profound deficiency of mature B cells and immunoglobulins. The lack of functional BTK prevents proper BCR signaling, halting B-cell development at the pre-B cell stage in the bone marrow. This explains the clinical presentation of recurrent bacterial infections, particularly with encapsulated organisms like *Streptococcus pneumoniae* and *Haemophilus influenzae*.
- **Other Pathways:**
    - **JAK-STAT pathway:** Primarily involved in cytokine signaling (e.g., IL-2, IFN-γ). Defects can cause immunodeficiencies (e.g., JAK3 deficiency in X-linked SCID), but the clinical picture (normal T cells, absent B cells) and specific gene mutation (*BTK*) do not fit.
    - **NF-κB pathway:** A central transcription factor activated by various stimuli, including BCR signaling, TLR signaling, and TNF. While BCR signaling activates NF-κB, the primary defect in XLA is upstream of NF-κB activation, specifically in the initial BCR signal transduction mediated by BTK.
    - **TLR signaling pathway:** Involved in innate immune responses to microbial components. Defects typically lead to susceptibility to viral infections or specific bacterial infections, but not the characteristic B-cell deficiency seen here.
    - **Complement activation pathway:** Part of the innate immune system, involving a cascade of proteins leading to opsonization, inflammation, and cell lysis. Defects cause increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria, but the primary defect here is in adaptive immunity (B-cell development).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **JAK-STAT pathway:** This pathway is crucial for cytokine receptor signaling, particularly for T-cell and NK-cell development and function. Mutations in JAK3 cause X-linked Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID), characterized by defects in both T and B cells (and NK cells). This patient has normal T cells, making JAK-STAT deficiency unlikely.
- (B): **NF-κB pathway:** This pathway is activated downstream of many receptors, including the BCR and TLRs. While BCR signaling leads to NF-κB activation, the primary defect in XLA is the inability to initiate this cascade due to the lack of BTK-mediated phosphorylation events upstream. Defects specifically in NF-κB components can cause immunodeficiencies (e.g., NEMO deficiency), but the clinical picture and *BTK* mutation point elsewhere.
- (D): **Toll-like receptor (TLR) signaling pathway:** TLRs are pattern recognition receptors involved in innate immunity. Defects in TLR signaling components can lead to susceptibility to specific infections, but they do not typically cause the profound B-cell deficiency seen in XLA.
- (E): **Complement activation pathway:** This pathway is part of the innate immune system. Deficiencies in complement components (e.g., C3, C5-C9) increase susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria, similar to antibody deficiencies. However, the clinical presentation here, particularly the absence of mature B cells and the *BTK* mutation, clearly indicates a primary B-cell developmental defect, not a complement deficiency.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) / BTK Deficiency | JAK3 Deficiency (X-linked SCID) | NF-κB Deficiency (e.g., NEMO) | TLR Deficiency | Complement Deficiency |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------|-----------------------|
| **Gene Defect**         | *BTK*                                             | *JAK3*                          | *NFKB1*, *IKBKG* (NEMO)         | Various TLR genes | Various complement genes |
| **Inheritance**         | X-linked recessive                                | X-linked recessive               | X-linked dominant/recessive     | Autosomal       | Various                |
| **B-cell Count**        | Absent/Markedly Reduced                            | Absent/Markedly Reduced          | Normal/Reduced                  | Normal          | Normal                |
| **T-cell Count**        | Normal                                             | Absent/Markedly Reduced          | Normal                          | Normal          | Normal                |
| **NK-cell Count**       | Normal                                             | Absent/Markedly Reduced          | Normal                          | Normal          | Normal                |
| **Immunoglobulins**     | Absent/Markedly Reduced                            | Absent/Markedly Reduced          | Normal/Reduced                  | Normal          | Normal/Reduced        |
| **Primary Defect**      | BCR Signaling, B-cell Development                  | Cytokine Signaling, Lymphocyte Development | NF-κB Activation, Immune Regulation | Innate Immunity | Innate Immunity       |
| **Key Infections**      | Encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *S. pneumoniae*, *H. influenzae*) | Opportunistic infections (viral, fungal, bacterial) | Recurrent bacterial infections, ectodermal dysplasia | Viral infections | Encapsulated bacteria |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) is caused by mutations in the *BTK* gene, leading to a defect in the B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling pathway and subsequent failure of B-cell development. This results in absent mature B cells and immunoglobulins, causing susceptibility to encapsulated bacterial infections. Key differentials include other primary immunodeficiencies affecting B cells (e.g., common variable immunodeficiency - CVID, which typically presents later and has normal B cell numbers but impaired differentiation) and combined immunodeficiencies (e.g., SCID, which affects both T and B cells). Remember that XLA is specifically a B-cell defect due to impaired BCR signaling.

<a id='question-080'></a>

### Question 080: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 30 (production)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Defective T-cell development due to mutations in genes encoding transcription factors essential for thymic epithelial cell function and T-cell receptor rearrangement, leading to DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome). (T细胞发育缺陷，因编码胸腺上皮细胞功能和T细胞受体重排必需转录因子的基因突变，导致DiGeorge综合征（22q11.2缺失综合征）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 10-month-old infant presents with recurrent respiratory infections, including pneumonia and otitis media, starting around 3 months of age. The infant also has a history of feeding difficulties, failure to thrive, and hypocalcemic seizures. Physical examination reveals characteristic facial features including hypertelorism, a short philtrum, and a low-set mouth. Cardiac examination reveals a conotruncal heart defect, specifically Tetralogy of Fallot. Laboratory studies show lymphopenia, particularly a marked reduction in T lymphocytes (CD3+ cells), with relatively normal B lymphocyte (CD19+) and NK cell (CD56+) counts. Immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) are low, consistent with impaired T-cell help for B-cell antibody production. A genetic test confirms a heterozygous deletion on chromosome 22q11.2.

Which of the following is the MOST likely underlying defect responsible for the patient's immunodeficiency?

(A) Defective synthesis of the common gamma chain (γc) shared by multiple cytokine receptors.
(B) Impaired development of the thymus due to abnormal migration of neural crest cells.
(C) Defective expression of the CD40 ligand (CD40L) on activated T cells.
(D) Defective expression of the Bruton tyrosine kinase (BTK) in B lymphocytes.
(E) Defective expression of the NADPH oxidase complex in phagocytes.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent infections (respiratory):** Suggests an immunodeficiency, particularly affecting T-cell mediated immunity or antibody production (B-cell function).
- **Onset at 3 months:** Maternal antibodies wane around this time, unmasking underlying immunodeficiencies.
- **Feeding difficulties, failure to thrive, hypocalcemic seizures:** Classic features associated with hypoparathyroidism, often seen in DiGeorge syndrome due to parathyroid gland hypoplasia/aplasia.
- **Characteristic facial features (hypertelorism, short philtrum, low-set mouth):** Dysmorphic features strongly suggestive of DiGeorge syndrome.
- **Conotruncal heart defect (Tetralogy of Fallot):** Another common feature of DiGeorge syndrome, arising from abnormal development of the outflow tract.
- **Lymphopenia, marked reduction in T lymphocytes (CD3+):** Points directly to a T-cell deficiency.
- **Normal B and NK cells:** Suggests the defect is specific to T-cell development, not a general lymphoid defect.
- **Low immunoglobulins:** Secondary to lack of T-cell help for B-cell maturation and antibody production.
- **22q11.2 deletion:** Confirms the diagnosis of DiGeorge syndrome (also known as 22q11.2 deletion syndrome or velocardiofacial syndrome).
- **Question asks for the underlying defect:** Requires linking the clinical presentation (DiGeorge syndrome) to its immunological cause.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome):** Caused by a microdeletion on chromosome 22q11.2, affecting multiple genes, most notably *TBX1*.
- **TBX1 Gene:** Encodes a transcription factor crucial for the development of the third and fourth pharyngeal pouches during embryogenesis.
- **Third Pharyngeal Pouch:** Gives rise to the thymus and parathyroid glands.
- **Fourth Pharyngeal Pouch:** Gives rise to the ultimobranchial body, which contributes to the C cells of the thyroid gland.
- **Pathophysiology:** The deletion leads to abnormal development (hypoplasia or aplasia) of the thymus and parathyroid glands.
- **Thymic Hypoplasia/Aplasia:** Results in impaired T-cell development and maturation, leading to T-cell lymphopenia (especially CD4+ T cells, but affecting both CD4+ and CD8+). This is the primary cause of the immunodeficiency.
- **Parathyroid Hypoplasia/Aplasia:** Leads to hypoparathyroidism, causing hypocalcemia (manifesting as seizures).
- **Other features:** Cardiac defects (conotruncal anomalies like Tetralogy of Fallot, interrupted aortic arch), characteristic facial features, cleft palate, learning disabilities.
- **Immunological Consequence:** Lack of functional T cells impairs cell-mediated immunity and T-cell help for B cells, leading to low antibody levels and susceptibility to opportunistic infections (viral, fungal, intracellular bacteria) and common bacterial infections.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective synthesis of the common gamma chain (γc):** This causes X-linked Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (X-SCID). γc is essential for signaling through receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21. IL-7 is crucial for T-cell development, and IL-15 is important for NK cell development. Patients with γc deficiency have absent T cells and NK cells, but B cells are usually present (though non-functional without T-cell help). This does not fit the patient's profile of normal B and NK cells and the specific genetic finding (22q11.2 deletion).
- (C) **Defective expression of the CD40 ligand (CD40L):** This causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. CD40L on activated T cells binds to CD40 on B cells, macrophages, and dendritic cells, providing essential signals for B-cell class switching, somatic hypermutation, and isotype switching. Patients have normal T-cell numbers but cannot produce IgG, IgA, or IgE, resulting in high IgM levels and susceptibility to opportunistic infections. This does not fit the patient's T-cell lymphopenia or the genetic finding.
- (D) **Defective expression of the Bruton tyrosine kinase (BTK):** This causes X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA). BTK is essential for B-cell development beyond the pre-B cell stage. Patients have absent B cells and very low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes. T-cell numbers and function are normal. This does not fit the patient's T-cell lymphopenia or normal B-cell count.
- (E) **Defective expression of the NADPH oxidase complex in phagocytes:** This causes Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). NADPH oxidase is essential for generating superoxide radicals, which are used by phagocytes to kill ingested microbes. Patients have recurrent bacterial and fungal infections, often forming granulomas. Lymphocyte counts and immunoglobulin levels are typically normal. This does not fit the patient's T-cell lymphopenia or the specific constellation of features (cardiac defects, facial features, hypocalcemia).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) | X-SCID (γc deficiency) | Hyper-IgM (CD40L deficiency) | XLA (BTK deficiency) | CGD (NADPH oxidase defect) |
|--------------------------|--------------------------------------|------------------------|-----------------------------|----------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Genetic Basis**        | 22q11.2 deletion (*TBX1*)           | X-linked (IL2RG)       | X-linked (CD40LG)           | X-linked (BTK)      | X-linked or AR (CYBB, etc.) |
| **T Cells**              | Decreased (especially CD4+)         | Absent                 | Normal number               | Normal               | Normal                      |
| **B Cells**              | Normal number                       | Normal number          | Normal number               | Absent               | Normal                      |
| **NK Cells**             | Normal number                       | Absent                 | Normal number               | Normal               | Normal                      |
| **Immunoglobulins**      | Low (all isotypes)                  | Low (all isotypes)     | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE    | Very Low (all)       | Normal                      |
| **Key Clinical Features**| Cardiac defects, Facial features, Hypocalcemia, Thymic hypoplasia | Severe infections early | Opportunistic infections | Recurrent bacterial infections | Granulomas, recurrent infections |
| **Primary Defect**       | Thymic development                  | T-cell development     | B-cell class switching      | B-cell development   | Phagocyte killing           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) is characterized by thymic hypoplasia leading to T-cell deficiency, often accompanied by cardiac defects, hypocalcemia (due to parathyroid hypoplasia), and characteristic facial features. The underlying defect is abnormal development of structures derived from the third and fourth pharyngeal pouches due to mutations in genes like *TBX1*. Differentiating it from other primary immunodeficiencies requires recognizing the specific combination of clinical features (cardiac, facial, endocrine) and the characteristic immunological profile (T-cell lymphopenia with normal B and NK cells).

<a id='question-081'></a>

### Question 081: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 31 (Symptoms)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the clinical manifestations and underlying immunological defects associated with specific primary immunodeficiencies, particularly those affecting T-cell function and cytokine signaling, leading to susceptibility to opportunistic infections and autoimmunity. (考察特定原发性免疫缺陷的临床表现和潜在的免疫缺陷，特别是影响T细胞功能和细胞因子信号传导的缺陷，导致对机会性感染和自身免疫的易感性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent, severe bacterial infections (pneumonia, otitis media, sinusitis) starting at 6 months of age, after maternal antibodies waned. He also has persistent oral thrush unresponsive to standard antifungal therapy and failure to thrive. Physical examination reveals lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly. Laboratory tests show markedly reduced T-cell counts (CD3+ < 500/µL), normal B-cell counts (CD19+), and absent delayed-type hypersensitivity (DTH) skin reactions to Candida antigen. Flow cytometry reveals normal expression of CD3, CD4, and CD8 surface markers on the few remaining T cells, but these cells fail to proliferate in response to mitogenic stimulation (PHA) or IL-2. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *JAK3* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological defect in this patient?

(A) Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling leading to impaired positive selection in the thymus.
(B) Impaired B-cell maturation due to defective immunoglobulin heavy chain rearrangement.
(C) Defective cytokine receptor signaling preventing downstream activation pathways.
(D) Defective phagocyte chemotaxis due to mutations in integrin subunits.
(E) Defective complement activation leading to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age of onset (6 months):** Suggests a defect in cell-mediated immunity, as maternal antibodies provide passive immunity for the first few months of life. Recurrent bacterial infections after this period point towards a T-cell defect.
- **Recurrent severe bacterial infections:** Common in T-cell deficiencies (e.g., *S. aureus*, *S. pneumoniae*, *H. influenzae*).
- **Persistent oral thrush (Candida):** Indicates impaired cell-mediated immunity against fungi, a hallmark of T-cell defects.
- **Failure to thrive, lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly:** Non-specific signs often seen in severe immunodeficiencies, potentially due to chronic infections or autoimmune phenomena.
- **Markedly reduced T-cell counts (CD3+ < 500/µL):** Confirms a severe T-cell deficiency.
- **Normal B-cell counts (CD19+):** Suggests the primary defect is not in B-cell development itself, although T-cell help is crucial for optimal B-cell function.
- **Absent DTH skin reactions:** DTH is a T-cell mediated response, confirming the functional defect in cell-mediated immunity.
- **Normal surface CD markers but failed proliferation:** Indicates that T cells are present but cannot respond to signals for activation and proliferation, pointing towards a defect in intracellular signaling pathways rather than cell surface receptor expression or T-cell development itself.
- **Homozygous mutation in the *JAK3* gene:** This is the definitive clue. JAK3 is a tyrosine kinase essential for signaling downstream of several cytokine receptors, including those for the common gamma chain (γc) cytokines (IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, IL-21).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **JAK3 Function:** Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) is a non-receptor tyrosine kinase that associates with the common gamma chain (γc) of several cytokine receptors. Upon cytokine binding, JAK3 is activated and phosphorylates Signal Transducer and Activator of Transcription (STAT) proteins. Phosphorylated STATs dimerize, translocate to the nucleus, and regulate gene transcription involved in lymphocyte development, activation, and function.
- **JAK3 Deficiency (X-linked SCID):** Mutations in *JAK3* cause X-linked Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (X-SCID). This condition is characterized by a profound deficiency in T cells and NK cells, and often B cells as well (though B cells may be present but non-functional due to lack of T-cell help). The defect lies in the inability of lymphocytes expressing γc-dependent receptors (including the IL-2 receptor and IL-7 receptor) to transduce signals necessary for their development and function. IL-7 is crucial for T-cell development, and IL-2 is critical for T-cell proliferation and activation. The lack of signaling prevents T-cell maturation in the thymus and subsequent activation in the periphery.
- **Clinical Manifestations of X-SCID:** Severe, recurrent infections starting early in life (often within the first few months) due to opportunistic pathogens (e.g., *Pneumocystis jirovecii*, CMV, Candida) and common bacteria/viruses. Failure to thrive is common.
- **Mechanism of Option C:** The *JAK3* mutation directly impairs the intracellular signaling pathway downstream of cytokine receptors that utilize the common gamma chain. This prevents the phosphorylation of STAT proteins and subsequent gene transcription required for lymphocyte development (especially T cells, which rely on IL-7 signaling) and activation (which relies on IL-2 signaling). This aligns perfectly with the patient's clinical presentation and genetic findings.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling leading to impaired positive selection in the thymus.** This describes defects in the TCR complex itself or associated signaling molecules (e.g., ZAP-70 deficiency). While it causes T-cell deficiency, the patient's T cells express normal surface markers (CD3, CD4, CD8), and the defect is in proliferation *after* stimulation, not necessarily in initial development or TCR expression. The *JAK3* mutation specifically affects cytokine receptor signaling, not TCR signaling directly.
- (B) **Impaired B-cell maturation due to defective immunoglobulin heavy chain rearrangement.** This describes defects like X-linked agammaglobulinemia (Bruton's tyrosine kinase deficiency) or common variable immunodeficiency (CVID). These primarily affect B-cell development and antibody production. The patient has normal B-cell counts, and the primary problem is T-cell deficiency.
- (D) **Defective phagocyte chemotaxis due to mutations in integrin subunits.** This describes Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency (LAD). LAD affects neutrophil function, leading to recurrent bacterial infections, impaired wound healing, and delayed umbilical cord separation. It does not primarily affect T-cell numbers or function, nor does it explain the persistent thrush or the specific genetic finding (*JAK3* mutation).
- (E) **Defective complement activation leading to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria.** This describes complement deficiencies (e.g., C3 deficiency, C5-C9 deficiency). While these increase susceptibility to certain bacterial infections (especially encapsulated ones), they do not typically cause severe T-cell deficiency, persistent thrush, or failure to thrive to this extent. The underlying defect is in the complement cascade, not lymphocyte signaling.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | X-linked SCID (JAK3 Defect) | DiGeorge Syndrome (TCR Defect) | LAD (Integrin Defect) | CVID (B-cell Defect) | Complement Deficiency |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------- | :---------------------------- | :-------------------- | :------------------- | :-------------------- |
| **Primary Cell Type**   | T cells, NK cells           | T cells                      | Neutrophils           | B cells              | N/A                   |
| **T-cell Count**        | Severely reduced            | Reduced                      | Normal                | Normal               | Normal                |
| **B-cell Count**        | Often reduced/non-functional | Normal                       | Normal                | Reduced              | Normal                |
| **Key Infections**      | Opportunistic (PCP, CMV, Candida), bacterial | Viral, fungal             | Bacterial (skin, mucosal) | Encapsulated bacteria | Encapsulated bacteria |
| **Thrush**              | Common, persistent          | Possible                     | Rare                  | Rare                 | Rare                  |
| **Lymph Nodes/Spleen**  | Absent/small                | Variable                     | Normal                | Variable             | Normal                |
| **Genetic Defect**      | *JAK3* (X-linked)           | *TBX1*, *22q11.2 deletion*    | Integrin subunits      | Various              | Complement genes      |
| **Mechanism**           | Defective cytokine signaling | Defective T-cell development | Defective chemotaxis  | Defective B-cell maturation | Defective opsonization/lysis |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
X-linked SCID due to *JAK3* mutations is a severe primary immunodeficiency characterized by profound T-cell and NK-cell deficiency due to impaired cytokine receptor signaling. Key features include early-onset severe opportunistic and bacterial infections, persistent thrush, and failure to thrive. Differentiating it from other T-cell deficiencies (like DiGeorge) requires understanding the specific signaling pathway defect (cytokine vs. TCR) and the genetic basis. Remember JAK3 is crucial for signaling downstream of the common gamma chain (γc) receptors for IL-2, IL-7, etc.

<a id='question-082'></a>

### Question 082: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 32 (Recurrent)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular defect in Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD), specifically the inability of phagocytes to produce reactive oxygen species (ROS) due to a defective NADPH oxidase complex, leading to recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms. (本题考察慢性肉芽肿病 (CGD) 的分子缺陷，即由于 NADPH 氧化酶复合体缺陷，吞噬细胞无法产生活性氧 (ROS)，导致感染 catalase 阳性微生物的复发。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 2-week history of fever, cough, and shortness of breath. His mother reports that he has had recurrent episodes of pneumonia, skin abscesses, and lymphadenitis since infancy, often requiring hospitalization. He was born full-term without complications. Family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died at age 10 from overwhelming sepsis. Physical examination reveals a cachectic child with multiple draining skin lesions on his trunk and extremities, cervical lymphadenopathy, and crackles in both lung bases. Laboratory studies show leukocytosis with neutrophilia. A chest X-ray confirms bilateral pneumonia with multiple cavitary lesions. Sputum culture grows *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Aspergillus fumigatus*. A nitroblue tetrazolium (NBT) test is performed on neutrophils, which fail to reduce the dye to a blue formazan precipitate upon stimulation with phorbol myristate acetate (PMA).

Which of the following molecular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Defective synthesis of the common gamma chain (γc) shared by multiple cytokine receptors.
(B) Impaired DNA double-strand break repair leading to increased chromosomal instability and susceptibility to infections.
(C) Defective assembly or function of the NADPH oxidase complex, preventing the respiratory burst.
(D) Defective expression of CD40 ligand (CD40L) on activated T cells, impairing isotype switching in B cells.
(E) Defective expression of the CD154 protein, leading to impaired T-cell activation and cytokine production.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent infections since infancy:** Suggests a primary immunodeficiency. The types of infections (pneumonia, skin abscesses, lymphadenitis) point towards a defect in phagocytic function or T-cell immunity.
- **Specific pathogens:** *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Aspergillus fumigatus* are catalase-positive organisms. Catalase breaks down hydrogen peroxide, which is normally used by phagocytes to kill ingested microbes. A defect in generating hydrogen peroxide would make the patient particularly susceptible to these organisms.
- **Family history:** Maternal uncle dying young from sepsis suggests an X-linked inheritance pattern, common in some primary immunodeficiencies (like CGD and X-linked agammaglobulinemia).
- **Physical exam:** Cachexia, draining skin lesions, lymphadenopathy, and cavitary lung lesions are consistent with chronic granulomatous inflammation and abscess formation, characteristic of CGD.
- **NBT test:** The failure of neutrophils to reduce NBT dye indicates a defect in the respiratory burst, the process by which phagocytes generate superoxide radicals (a type of ROS) to kill ingested pathogens. This is the hallmark diagnostic test for CGD.
- **Conclusion:** The clinical presentation (recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms, granuloma formation), family history (suggesting X-linked inheritance), and positive NBT test strongly point towards Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD):** CGD is a primary immunodeficiency characterized by defects in the NADPH oxidase enzyme complex. This complex is located in the phagolysosome membrane of phagocytic cells (neutrophils, macrophages, monocytes) and is responsible for generating superoxide radicals (O2-) during the respiratory burst.
- **NADPH Oxidase Complex:** This multi-subunit enzyme complex catalyzes the reduction of molecular oxygen to superoxide. The key components include gp91phox (NOX2, encoded by the *CYBB* gene on the X chromosome, most common defect), p22phox, p47phox, p67phox, and p40phox.
- **Respiratory Burst:** Upon phagocytic stimulation (e.g., by bacteria or fungi), the NADPH oxidase subunits assemble at the phagosome membrane. Electrons are transferred from NADPH in the cytoplasm to molecular oxygen within the phagosome, generating superoxide (O2-). Superoxide is then converted to other ROS, such as hydrogen peroxide (H2O2) by superoxide dismutase (SOD), and hypochlorous acid (HOCl) by myeloperoxidase (MPO). These ROS are crucial for killing ingested microbes.
- **Pathophysiology of CGD:** In CGD, the defective NADPH oxidase complex cannot generate sufficient ROS. This impairs the ability of phagocytes to kill certain ingested pathogens, particularly catalase-positive organisms like *S. aureus*, *Aspergillus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, and *Nocardia*. Catalase breaks down H2O2, preventing its accumulation and subsequent killing by MPO. Phagocytes can still phagocytose these organisms, but they cannot effectively kill them, leading to persistent infection, granuloma formation (attempted containment), and abscesses.
- **Inheritance:** Most cases (~70%) are X-linked recessive due to mutations in the *CYBB* gene encoding gp91phox. Autosomal recessive forms exist due to mutations in genes encoding other subunits (e.g., *CYBA*, *NCF1*, *NCF2*, *NCF4*).
- **Diagnosis:** The NBT test (or the more sensitive dihydrorhodamine (DHR) flow cytometry assay) assesses the ability of neutrophils to produce ROS. Failure to reduce NBT or oxidize DHR confirms the diagnosis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective synthesis of the common gamma chain (γc) shared by multiple cytokine receptors:** This describes X-linked Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (X-SCID). Patients with X-SCID have profound defects in T-cell and NK-cell development due to impaired signaling through receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21. They present early in life with severe, opportunistic infections (viral, fungal, bacterial) and failure to thrive, not typically with recurrent catalase-positive infections and granulomas. The NBT test would be normal in X-SCID.
- (B) **Impaired DNA double-strand break repair leading to increased chromosomal instability and susceptibility to infections:** This describes Nijmegen breakage syndrome (NBS), caused by mutations in the *NBN* gene. NBS is characterized by immunodeficiency (primarily affecting T cells), microcephaly, facial dysmorphism, and increased risk of lymphoma. While infections occur, the specific pattern (catalase-positive organisms, granulomas) and the NBT test result are not characteristic of NBS.
- (D) **Defective expression of CD40 ligand (CD40L) on activated T cells, impairing isotype switching in B cells:** This describes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal or elevated IgM levels but deficient IgG, IgA, and IgE due to the inability of T cells to provide help to B cells for class switching. They are susceptible to opportunistic infections, particularly *Pneumocystis jirovecii*. The NBT test would be normal, and granulomas/catalase-positive infections are not the primary feature.
- (E) **Defective expression of the CD154 protein, leading to impaired T-cell activation and cytokine production:** CD154 is another name for CD40L. Therefore, this option describes the same condition as option (D), X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. The reasoning for its incorrectness is identical to that for option (D).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | X-SCID (γc deficiency) | Nijmegen Breakage Syndrome (NBS) | X-linked Hyper-IgM (CD40L defect) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :-------------------- | :------------------------------ | :-------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | NADPH oxidase complex               | γc signaling          | DNA double-strand break repair  | CD40L expression                  |
| **Cell Type Affected**  | Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages) | T cells, NK cells     | T cells                         | T cells, B cells                  |
| **Key Pathogen Susceptibility** | Catalase-positive bacteria/fungi | Opportunistic (viral, fungal, bacterial) | Opportunistic (bacterial, viral) | Opportunistic (esp. *Pneumocystis*) |
| **Characteristic Infections** | Pneumonia, abscesses, granulomas   | Severe, early onset   | Recurrent sinopulmonary         | *Pneumocystis* pneumonia          |
| **Typical Lab Findings** | Normal Ig levels, abnormal NBT/DHR | Low T/NK cells, low Ig | Variable Ig, chromosomal breaks | High IgM, low IgG/IgA/IgE         |
| **Inheritance**         | X-linked (most common), AR          | X-linked              | Autosomal recessive             | X-linked                          |
| **NBT Test**            | Negative                            | Positive              | Positive                        | Positive                          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CGD is a phagocytic defect characterized by recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms and granuloma formation due to impaired respiratory burst. The NBT test is diagnostic. Differentiate CGD from other primary immunodeficiencies based on affected cell types, specific pathogen susceptibility, immunoglobulin levels, and diagnostic test results (e.g., NBT, lymphocyte counts, CD40L expression). Remember the key pathogens for CGD: *S. aureus*, *Aspergillus*, *Serratia*, *Burkholderia*, *Nocardia*.
