<a id='question-099'></a>

### Question 099: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 49 (defects;)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular defect in Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD), specifically the inability of phagocytes to produce reactive oxygen species (ROS) due to defects in the NADPH oxidase complex, leading to impaired killing of catalase-positive organisms. (本题考察慢性肉芽肿病 (CGD) 的分子缺陷，即由于 NADPH 氧化酶复合体缺陷，吞噬细胞无法产生活性氧 (ROS)，从而导致对 catalase 阳性微生物的杀伤能力受损。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 10-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 3-week history of recurrent, severe bacterial and fungal infections, including pneumonia, skin abscesses, and lymphadenitis. His mother reports that he has had multiple hospitalizations for similar infections since infancy. He has a history of granuloma formation in the lungs and lymph nodes noted on prior imaging. Family history is significant for a similarly affected maternal uncle who died in his 20s from overwhelming sepsis. Physical examination reveals multiple draining skin abscesses, hepatosplenomegaly, and cervical lymphadenopathy. Laboratory studies show leukocytosis with neutrophilia. A skin biopsy from an abscess reveals numerous neutrophils surrounding a central area of necrosis, but the neutrophils lack the ability to kill the ingested bacteria. Nitroblue tetrazolium (NBT) test is negative, indicating a failure of neutrophils to reduce NBT dye. Flow cytometry analysis of neutrophils shows normal expression of CD11/CD18 (integrin) and CD16 (FcγRIII). Which of the following molecular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Defective synthesis of the common gamma chain (γc) shared by IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21 receptors.
(B) Defective expression of CD40 ligand (CD40L) on activated T cells.
(C) Defective expression of the gp91phox subunit of the NADPH oxidase complex.
(D) Defective expression of the CD154 (CD40L) receptor (CD40) on antigen-presenting cells.
(E) Defective expression of the Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) enzyme.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe bacterial and fungal infections since infancy:** Suggests a primary immunodeficiency affecting innate immunity, particularly phagocyte function.
- **Granuloma formation:** Characteristic feature of Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) due to the body's attempt to wall off organisms that phagocytes cannot kill.
- **Family history:** Suggests a genetic basis, likely X-linked or autosomal recessive inheritance.
- **Skin abscesses, pneumonia, lymphadenitis:** Common manifestations of CGD caused by catalase-positive organisms (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, *Aspergillus* species).
- **Neutrophils present but unable to kill bacteria:** Points to a defect in the killing mechanism within phagocytes, rather than chemotaxis or phagocytosis itself.
- **Negative NBT test:** The NBT test assesses the respiratory burst activity of neutrophils. A negative result indicates a failure to produce superoxide radicals, a key component of the oxidative killing mechanism.
- **Normal CD11/CD18 and CD16 expression:** Rules out Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency (LAD) type 1 (CD11/CD18 defect) and defects in Fc receptor function (like LAD type 3, which affects CD16).
- **The combination of recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms, granuloma formation, and a negative NBT test is pathognomonic for CGD.**

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD):** A primary immunodeficiency characterized by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex, which is responsible for generating superoxide radicals (O2-) and other reactive oxygen species (ROS) during the respiratory burst in phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages).
- **NADPH Oxidase Complex:** A multi-subunit enzyme complex located in the phagosomal membrane. In humans, it consists of five subunits: gp91phox (NOX2, encoded by *CYBB* gene, X-linked), p22phox (encoded by *CYBA* gene, autosomal recessive), p47phox (encoded by *NCF1* gene, autosomal recessive), p67phox (encoded by *NCF2* gene, autosomal recessive), and p40phox (encoded by *NCF4* gene, autosomal recessive).
- **Mechanism of Defect:** Mutations in any of the genes encoding these subunits can lead to CGD. The most common form is X-linked (gp91phox defect), accounting for ~70% of cases. The defect prevents the assembly and activation of the functional NADPH oxidase complex.
- **Consequence of Defect:** Phagocytes can engulf microbes but cannot effectively kill catalase-positive bacteria and fungi because they lack the oxidative burst. Catalase-positive organisms break down the small amounts of hydrogen peroxide (H2O2) produced by other pathways, preventing the formation of toxic hydroxyl radicals. The inability to kill these organisms leads to persistent inflammation, granuloma formation, and recurrent infections.
- **NBT Test:** A diagnostic test for CGD. Neutrophils are incubated with NBT dye and stimulated (e.g., with PMA or bacteria). Normal neutrophils reduce the yellow NBT dye to blue formazan precipitate due to superoxide production. In CGD, neutrophils fail to reduce NBT, remaining yellow.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** Other phagocyte defects include Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency (LAD), Chediak-Higashi syndrome, and specific granule deficiency. However, the clinical picture (catalase-positive infections, granulomas) and the negative NBT test strongly point to CGD.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective synthesis of the common gamma chain (γc):** This defect causes X-linked Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (X-SCID). Patients present early in life with severe opportunistic infections (viral, fungal, bacterial) due to profound T cell and NK cell deficiency. B cells are present but non-functional. This does not fit the clinical picture of recurrent bacterial/fungal infections with granulomas and a specific phagocyte defect.
- (B) **Defective expression of CD40 ligand (CD40L) on activated T cells:** This defect causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have recurrent bacterial infections (especially encapsulated organisms) due to impaired class switching in B cells and defective T cell-dependent B cell activation. T cell function is otherwise normal. Granulomas and negative NBT test are not features of Hyper-IgM syndrome.
- (D) **Defective expression of the CD154 (CD40L) receptor (CD40) on antigen-presenting cells:** This is an autosomal recessive form of Hyper-IgM syndrome, clinically similar to the X-linked form caused by CD40L deficiency. It also does not fit the clinical picture of CGD.
- (E) **Defective expression of the Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) enzyme:** This defect causes X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA). Patients have recurrent bacterial infections (especially encapsulated organisms) starting after 6 months of age due to the absence of mature B cells and antibodies. T cell function and phagocyte function (including NBT test) are normal. Granulomas are not a feature.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | X-linked SCID (γc defect) | X-linked Hyper-IgM (CD40L defect) | XLA (BTK defect) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :----------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :--------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | NADPH oxidase complex (gp91phox most common) | Common γc chain          | CD40L on T cells                  | BTK enzyme       |
| **Affected Cells**      | Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages) | T cells, NK cells        | B cells (class switch), Macrophages | B cells          |
| **Key Infections**      | Catalase+ bacteria, fungi           | Opportunistic (viral, fungal, bacterial) | Encapsulated bacteria            | Encapsulated bacteria |
| **Granulomas**          | Yes                                 | No                       | No                                | No               |
| **NBT Test**            | Negative                             | Normal                   | Normal                            | Normal           |
| **Typical Age of Onset** | Infancy/Childhood                   | Infancy                  | Childhood                         | >6 months        |
| **Inheritance**         | X-linked (most common), AR           | X-linked                 | X-linked                          | X-linked         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) is a primary immunodeficiency caused by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex, leading to impaired phagocyte killing of catalase-positive organisms. The classic presentation includes recurrent bacterial and fungal infections, granuloma formation, and a negative NBT test. Key differentials include other phagocyte defects (LAD, Chediak-Higashi) and antibody deficiencies (XLA, Hyper-IgM), which typically do not present with granulomas or a negative NBT test. Remember the specific defect in CGD involves the respiratory burst pathway.

<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 2 Focus 50 (absent)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular defect and resulting functional consequence in Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD), specifically the inability of phagocytes to produce reactive oxygen species (ROS) due to a defect in the NADPH oxidase complex, leading to recurrent bacterial and fungal infections. (理解慢性肉芽肿病 (CGD) 的分子缺陷及其功能后果，即吞噬细胞由于 NADPH 氧化酶复合物缺陷而无法产生活性氧 (ROS)，导致反复的细菌和真菌感染。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 2-week history of fever, cough, and shortness of breath. He has a history of recurrent pneumonia, skin abscesses, and lymphadenitis since infancy, often requiring hospitalization. His mother reports that the infections are typically caused by organisms like *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, and *Aspergillus* species. Family history is significant for a similarly affected maternal uncle who died in his 20s from overwhelming sepsis. Physical examination reveals a cachectic child with multiple draining skin lesions and cervical lymphadenopathy. Chest X-ray shows bilateral pulmonary infiltrates and cavitation. Laboratory studies show leukocytosis with neutrophilia. A skin biopsy from an abscess reveals numerous neutrophils surrounding necrotic tissue, but these neutrophils lack the ability to kill the bacteria effectively. Flow cytometry analysis of neutrophils shows normal expression of CD11b/CD18 (LFA-1) and CD16 (FcγRIII), ruling out Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency (LAD) and FcγRIII deficiency, respectively. Which of the following intracellular pathways is most likely defective in this patient?

(A) Lysosomal trafficking and fusion
(B) Phagosome maturation and acidification
(C) Reactive oxygen species (ROS) production
(D) Complement receptor-mediated phagocytosis
(E) T-cell receptor signaling and activation

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 5-year-old boy with recurrent, severe infections (pneumonia, skin abscesses, lymphadenitis) since infancy. This points towards a primary immunodeficiency affecting innate immunity, particularly phagocytes.
- **Infecting Organisms:** *S. aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *B. cepacia*, *Aspergillus*. These are catalase-positive organisms (bacteria and fungi) that are typically killed by phagocytes via the respiratory burst mechanism. The specific pattern of infections is highly suggestive of CGD.
- **Family History:** Affected maternal uncle suggests an X-linked inheritance pattern, which is common for CGD (due to mutations in the *CYBB* gene encoding gp91phox).
- **Physical Exam:** Cachexia, draining skin lesions, lymphadenopathy, pulmonary infiltrates/cavitation are consistent with chronic, severe infections.
- **Neutrophil Function:** Skin biopsy shows neutrophils surrounding necrotic tissue but failing to kill bacteria. This indicates a defect in the *killing* mechanism within the phagocyte, not necessarily phagocytosis itself.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Normal CD11b/CD18 (LFA-1) rules out LAD (defect in adhesion). Normal CD16 (FcγRIII) rules out FcγRIII deficiency (defect in opsonization-mediated killing).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the defective intracellular pathway. Given the clinical picture and exclusion of other phagocyte defects, the respiratory burst pathway (ROS production) is the most likely culprit.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD):** A primary immunodeficiency characterized by defects in the NADPH oxidase enzyme complex.
- **NADPH Oxidase Function:** This multi-subunit enzyme complex (located in the phagosomal membrane) is responsible for generating superoxide radicals (O2•−) by transferring electrons from NADPH to molecular oxygen (O2). Superoxide is then converted to other ROS, such as hydrogen peroxide (H2O2) and hypochlorous acid (HOCl), which are potent microbicidal agents.
- **Molecular Defect:** Mutations in genes encoding subunits of the NADPH oxidase complex (e.g., *CYBB* for gp91phox, *CYBA* for p22phox, *NCF1*, *NCF2*, *NCF4*). The most common form is X-linked (gp91phox defect).
- **Pathophysiology:** Phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages) can engulf pathogens (phagocytosis) but cannot effectively kill catalase-positive organisms due to the inability to produce ROS. This leads to the formation of granulomas (attempted containment) and recurrent infections.
- **Infections:** Catalase-positive bacteria (*S. aureus*, *Serratia*, *Burkholderia*, *Nocardia*) and fungi (*Aspergillus*, *Candida*) are particularly problematic because they produce catalase, which breaks down any H2O2 produced by the host cells, preventing its use for killing.
- **Diagnosis:** Dihydrorhodamine (DHR) test or Nitroblue Tetrazolium (NBT) test measures the ability of neutrophils to produce ROS. Flow cytometry can assess NADPH oxidase subunit expression. Genetic testing confirms the specific mutation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Lysosomal trafficking and fusion:** Defects in lysosomal trafficking (e.g., Chediak-Higashi syndrome) lead to impaired fusion of lysosomes with phagosomes, preventing the delivery of lysosomal enzymes to the site of killing. While this causes phagocyte dysfunction, the characteristic infections and specific organisms differ from CGD. Chediak-Higashi also involves oculocutaneous albinism and giant granules in leukocytes.
- (B) **Phagosome maturation and acidification:** Defects in phagosome maturation (e.g., some forms of LAD, specific granule deficiency) impair the acidification of the phagosome, which is necessary for the activity of certain lysosomal enzymes. This is distinct from the respiratory burst defect in CGD.
- (D) **Complement receptor-mediated phagocytosis:** Defects in complement receptors (e.g., CR3 deficiency) impair the ability of phagocytes to bind and internalize opsonized pathogens. While phagocytosis might be affected, the primary defect in CGD is the *killing* mechanism *after* phagocytosis, and complement-mediated phagocytosis is generally intact.
- (E) **T-cell receptor signaling and activation:** Defects in T-cell receptor signaling (e.g., ZAP-70 deficiency) lead to impaired T-cell activation and function, resulting in susceptibility to viral, fungal, and intracellular bacterial infections, but not the specific pattern seen in CGD (catalase-positive organisms). This affects adaptive immunity, not the primary phagocyte killing defect seen here.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | Chediak-Higashi Syndrome | Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency (LAD) | FcγRIII Deficiency |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :------------------------ | :--------------------------------- | :----------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | NADPH oxidase complex (ROS production) | Lysosomal trafficking     | Integrin (CD11b/CD18) expression   | FcγRIII (CD16)     |
| **Cell Type Affected**  | Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages) | Phagocytes, Melanocytes   | Leukocytes                         | Neutrophils        |
| **Key Organisms**       | Catalase+ bacteria/fungi (*S. aureus*, *Aspergillus*) | *S. aureus*, *S. pyogenes* | *S. aureus*, *S. pyogenes*, *E. coli* | *S. aureus*, *S. pneumoniae*, *Candida* |
| **Characteristic Findings** | Granulomas, recurrent abscesses, cavitation | Albinism, giant granules, recurrent pyogenic infections | Delayed umbilical cord separation, recurrent infections, impaired wound healing | Recurrent *S. aureus* infections, impaired opsonization-mediated killing |
| **Diagnostic Test**     | DHR/NBT test                       | Electron microscopy       | Flow cytometry (CD11b/CD18)        | Flow cytometry (CD16) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CGD is a primary immunodeficiency caused by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex, leading to impaired ROS production and susceptibility to catalase-positive organisms. Differentiate CGD from other phagocyte defects like LAD (adhesion), Chediak-Higashi (lysosomal trafficking), and FcγRIII deficiency (opsonization-mediated killing) based on clinical presentation, specific organisms, and diagnostic tests (DHR/NBT for CGD, flow cytometry for LAD/FcγRIII deficiency, EM for Chediak-Higashi).
